[penguin] output cap clamped hard: max_tokens 256000 -> 122883 (context_window 128000, estimated input 4093)
Let me analyze this FEAL-like cipher.

The cipher:
- 64-bit block split into left (32-bit) and right (32-bit).
- Pre-round mixing: `right ^= left`
- 4 rounds of Feistel:
  ```
  mixed = f_function(right ^ key[round_num]);
  temp = right;
  right = left ^ mixed;
  left = temp;
  ```
- Post-round mixing: `left ^= right`
- Return `merge(right, left)`.

Let me examine the F function.

```
uint32_t f_function(uint32_t input_val) {
    uint8_t x[4];
    uint8_t y[4];
    for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
        x[i] = input_val & 0xFF;
        input_val >>= 8;
    }
    y[2] = g_function(x[3] ^ x[2], x[1] ^ x[0], 1);
    y[3] = g_function(x[3], y[2], 0);
    y[1] = g_function(x[1] ^ x[0], y[2], 0);
    y[0] = g_function(x[0], y[1], 1);
    for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
        output |= (uint32_t)y[i] << (8 * i);
    }
    return output;
}
```

Note byte order: x[i] = input & 0xFF (LSB first), so x[0] is the least significant byte of input_val_map. Output: y[i] << (8*i), so y[0] is least significant byte of output. So bytes are LSB-first: input = x0 | x1<<8 | x2<<16 | x3<<24; output = y0 | y1<<8 | y2<<16 | y3<<24.

g_function(a, b, x) = ROL2((a + b + x) mod 256), where ROL2 is rotate left by 2. Addition mod 256.

This is a nonlinear function because of the modular addition. "Linear attack" in FEAL context is a known technique: FEAL (the original cipher) had a linear attack where one constructs an equivalent linear relation for the F function using the fact that addition mod 2^8 followed by rotation... Hmm.

Actually, FEAL's "Linear Attack" was a known method (by Biham and Shamir? or "Gilbert"). It recovers the extended keys. The trick: treat F as a linear transformation for a chosen subset? Actually, I recall "linear cryptanalysis" and there is an explicit FEAL attack: "The linear attack on FEAL" where you use the fact that F(x) = F(x xor 0x...) ... Hmm.

Very interesting! Mask `0x80808080` gives probability 1.0 with output difference `0x02000002`. This is an exact linear relation:

F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x80808080) = 0x02000002 for ALL x.

Let me verify this for sure. That's a perfect linear approximation with probability 1! This is likely the key to the "linear attack".

Let me check the relation more carefully. Also, there may be other exact relations. For example mask 0x80808080 => output 0x02000002. This is a fixed point of the linear differential: with input difference 0x80808080, output difference is 0x02000002, with probability 1.

This means F(x) = F(x ^ 0x80808080) ^ 0x02000002.

So the F function is "linear" with respect to this particular mask. This kind of property is exactly what powers the FEAL linear attack: you can derive linear relations through the rounds and recover round key XORs.

Let me verify the property exactly with an exhaustive check over all 32-bit inputs... that's 4 billion, too much in Python but we can do random test with large sample. Already 50000 tests gave probability 1.0. Let me do a more rigorous verification of the relation and look for other relations.

Let me analyze the F function byte structure to prove this relation analytically:

g_function(a, b, x) = rol2[(a + b + x) mod 256].

Consider flipping the high bit (bit 7) of a and/or b. Since a+b is mod 256, adding 0x80 each... Let's see: (a+0x80) + b + x mod 256 = (a+b+x+0x80) mod 256. Rotate left 2 of a value differs... Let v = a+b+x mod 256ahan, v' = (v + 0x80) mod 256. Compare rol2(v) and rol2(v'): rol2(v) = (v<<2 | v>>6). v and v' differ in the top bit: v' = v ^ 0x80 if no overflow wrap? Actually adding 0x80 mod 256: if v < 0x80, v' = v+0x80; if v>=0x80, v' = v-0x80 = v ^ 0x80. So v' = v ^ 0x80 always! Adding 0x80 mod 256 simply flips the MSB because 0x80 is self-inverse: v + 0x80 mod 256 = (v + 128) mod 256 = flips bit 7.

So g(a ^ 0x80, b, x) = rol2[(a ^ 0x80) + b + x] = rol2[v ^ 0x80] where v = a+b+x mod 256.

Now rol2(v ^ 0x80): since rotation left 2 moves bit 7 to bit 1. Specifically rol2(x) = ((x << 2) | (x >> 6)) & 0xFF. If x' = x ^ 0x80 (bit7 flipped), rol2(x') = rol2(x) ^ 0x02 (since bit7 → bit1 after ROL2, i.e. 0x80 << 2 = 0x200 wraps to 0x02).

Let me verify: rol2(0x80) = (0x80 << 2 | 0x80 >> 6) = (0x200 | 0x02) & 0xFF = 0x02. Yes! So rol2(v ^ 0x80) = rol2(v) ^ 0x02.

Therefore g(a ^ 0x80, b, x) = g(a, b, x) ^ 0x02.
Similarly g(a, b ^ 0x80, x) = g(a,b,x) ^ 0x02.

So XOR of 0x80 in any of the "a" or "b" inputs to g results in XOR of 0x02 in output. This explains the exact differential for 0x80808080 input mask.

Now let's analyze F: input bytes x0..x3. XOR input with 0x80808080 flips top bit of each byte.

Derivation:
- y2 = g(x3^x2, x1^x0, 1). Flipping top bits of x3,x2,x1,x0: (x3^x2) gets top bit flipped (XOR of two flips = no change?) Wait: x3^x2 both have bit7 flipped => XOR unchanged! Indeed 0x80 ^ 0x80 = 0. So (x3^x2) is UNCHANGED. (x1^x0) also unchanged. So y2 is unchanged.

- y3 = g(x3, y2, 0). x3 bit7 flipped => y3 ^= 0x02.

- y1 = g(x1^x0, y2, 0). x1^x0 unchanged, y2 unchanged => y1 unchanged.

- y0 = g(x0, y1, 1). x0 bit7 flipped => y0 ^= 0x02.

So F(x ^ 0x80808080) = F(x) ^ (0x02 in byte 3) ^ (0x02 in byte 0) = F(x) ^ 0x02000002. Matches empirical (bytes: y3 << 24 | ... y0). Difference 0x02000002 = byte3=0x02, byte0=0x02.

Great, so we have an exact differential (probability 1) for input mask 0x80808080 → output mask 0x02000002.

This is exactly the "linear attack" material. With this exact low-weight linear relation, we can reduce the cipher to a linear system over GF(2) involving XOR of round keys (up to one bit per byte).

Actually let's carefully trace a differential through the cipher. We want input differences at each round's F to be 0x80808080 (so that we know the output diff of F is 0x02000002 regardless of key, because key XOR is crossed out: (R^K) differ then... wait.

Important: F((R ⊕ Δ) ⊕ K) vs F(R ⊕ K): input to F differs by Δ (since key is same). So if Δ = 0x80808080, then F output differs by 0x02000002 exactly.

So if we can make the difference in the right-half input to each round equal 0x80808080, the round output difference is the constant 0x02000002.

Then the evolution of the difference through the Feistel is linear:

Feistel round: L_{i+1} = R_i; R_{i+1} = L_i ⊕ F(R_i ⊕ K_i).

Let ΔL_i = L_i ⊕ L_i', ΔR_i = R_i ⊕ R_i'.

Round:
ΔL_{i+1} = ΔR_i
ΔR_{i+1} = ΔL_i ⊕ (F(R_i⊕K_i) ⊕ F(R_i'⊕K_i)) = ΔL_i ⊕ (0x02000002 if ΔR_i = 0x80808080).

So if we set ΔR_i = 0x80808080, then ΔR_{i+1} = ΔL_i ⊕ 0x02000002.

This yields a linear propagation (over GF(2) XOR) of differences through rounds. Since the keys cancel (same key used for both), the differential doesn't directly involve keys.

Define the F function differential:
F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x80808080) = 0x02000002.  (Eq L)

Now, consider the encryption structure. Let's compute the difference through two plaintexts that differ by a specific mask.

Let P = (L0, R0), P' = (L0 ⊕ a, R0 ⊕ b). We want to choose a, b so that differences propagate nicely.

Pre-mixing: A = R0 ⊕ L0; A' = R0' ⊕ L0' = (R0⊕b) ⊕ (L0⊕a) = A ⊕ (a ⊕ b). Let δA = A ⊕ A' = a ⊕ b.

Round 1: input to F is R_i... wait round 1: F input = (R0') ⊕ K0 = A ⊕ K0. Hmm careful: at round 1, F input = R0' ^ K0 where R0' = A.

Let me define difference variables per round for the "right input to F" (the value R before F in each round):

Round 0: input to F: I0 = A ⊕ K0. Difference δI0 = δA = a ⊕ b.
Output: O0 = F(I0). Round: R1 = L0 ⊕ O0; L1 = A.

δR1 = δL0 ⊕ δO0 = a ⊕ (F(I0) ⊕ F(I0')) where δI0 must be 0x80808080 to use the relation. Similarly δL1 = δA = a ⊕ b.

Round 1: input to F: I1 = R1 ⊕ K1. δI1 = δR1.
O1 = F(I1). R2 = L1 ⊕ O1; L2 = R1.
δL2 = δR1; δR2 = δL1 ⊕ δO1.

Round 2: I2 = R2 ⊕ K2; O2 = F(I2). R3 = L2 ⊕ O2; L3 = R2.
δR3 = δL2 ⊕ δO2; δL3 = δR2.

Round 3: I3 = R3 ⊕ K3; O3 = F(I3). R4 = L3 ⊕ O3; L4 = R3.
δR4 = δL3 ⊕ δO3; δL4 = δR3.

Post: L5 = L4 ⊕ R4. δL5 = δL4 ⊕ δR4.
Cipher C = (CL, CR) = (R4, L5). δCL = δR4, δCR = δL5.

To make each F input difference 0x80808080, we need:
δI0 = δA = a ⊕ b = 0x80808080
δI1 = δR1 = a ⊕ δO0, where δO0 = 0x02000002 (given δI0 = 0x80808080). So δI1 = a ⊕ 0x02000002. We want δI1 = 0x80808080, so a ⊕ 0x02000002 = 0x80808080 => a = 0x80808080 ⊕ 0x02000002 = 0x82808082.

δI2 = δR2 = δL1 ⊕ δO1 = (a ⊕ b) ⊕ 0x02000002 = 0x80808080 ⊕ 0x02000002 = 0x82808082. (want that = 0x80808080? No.)

δR1 = 0x80808080.
δL2 = δR1 = 0x80808080.
δR2 = δL1 ⊕ δO1 = 0x80808080 ⊕ 0x02000002 = 0x82808082.
δI2 = δR2 = 0x82808082. Not 0x80808080 → output difference of F unknown in general.

Consider masks that flip only byte3's top bit: Δin = 0x80000000. Then which bytes of output change? 
- y2 = g(x3^x2, x1^x0, 1): x3 top bit flips ⇒ (x3^x2) flips top bit ⇒ y2 ^= 0x02.
- y3 = g(x3, y2, 0): x3 flips top bit ⇒ y3 ^= 0x02; y2 also flips. So y3 has two sources: g(a^0x80, b, x) gives ^0x02; and g(a, b^0x02, x)? Wait y2 changed by 0x02, which is input as b to y3's g. Let's compute g(a, b ^ d, x) - g(a,b,x) for d=0x02: need to know how adding 0x02 to b affects the sum. (a + b + x + d) mod 256 vs (a+b+x) mod 256. This is not simply XOR d because adding 0x02 may carry. So output diff for flipping byte3 only is NOT constant (depends on carries).

So the exact linear differential only holds for flipping ALL four bytes' top bits simultaneously, reason: the XORs (x3^x2) and (x1^x0) cancel the flips, resulting in y2 unchangedessed, and y3/y0 each get one flip → deterministic output.

So the only exact differential might be input mask 0x80808080 and output 0x02000002)Skip.

Let me verify with an exhaustive-ish search over all masks that are "nice" (e.g., bytes all 0 or 0x80). But the differential only needs to be deterministic. Let me at least test the 8-byte masks with each byte 0x80/0x00. The masks 0x80808080 is the only one with all 4 bytes 0x80. Masks with fewer bytes might not be deterministic. Let me test.

Actually, let me step back. In the actual FEAL linear attack, they use MORE relations. There are several exact differentials? In FEAL, the F function satisfies more than one linear approximation. Let me analyze the byte-level relations more thoroughly.

Let me brute force the exact behavior: For each mask m measured over random 32-bit x, compute the set of output differences and their probabilities. If a mask yields a single output diff for all x, that's an exact linear relation. Let me search over a space of masks that are byte-uniform or low Hamming weight. Let me think analytically about which masks produce deterministic output differences.

The F function: 
y2 = rol2[(x3⊕x2) + (x1⊕x0) + 1]
y3 = rol2[x3 + y2]
y1 = rol2[(x1⊕x0) + y2]
y0 = rol2[x0 + y1 + 1]

Consider XORing input with mask m = (m0,m1,m2,m3) byte-wise. The internal variables change:
- Let u = x3⊕x2, v = x1⊕x0.
- Δu = m3⊕m2, Δv = m1⊕m0.
- y2 input sum: (u + v + 1). Output y2 changes depend on Δu, Δv in addition.
- y3 input: x3 + y2. Δ from m3 (flip) and Δy2.
- y1 input: v + y2; Δ from Δv and Δy2.
- y0 input: x0 + y1; Δ from m0 and Δy1.

For the output difference to be deterministic (independent of carries), we probably need each addition to have either no change or a "clean" flip that doesn't propagate carries. The clean flip is adding 0x80 (flips MSB). Adding other constants may create carries.

If Δu = Δv = 0 (i.e., m3=m2 and m1=m0), then y2 unchanged. Then y3's change comes from x3's flip m3 only:
- y3 changes deterministically iff m3 ∈ {0x00, 0x80} (adding 0x80 flips MSB; adding 0x00 no change). Other flips cause carries → not deterministic.
Similarly y1 unchanged (since v unchanged, y2 unchanged).
y0 changes from x0's flip m0 and y1 (unchanged). So deterministic iff m0 ∈ {0x00, 0x80}.

So for m3=m2 and m1=m0, and m0,m3 ∈ {0x00,0x80}, we get deterministic output. With each byte ∈ {0x00, 0x80} and m3=m2, m1=m0.

Given m3=m2 and m1=m0, and all bytes in {0,0x80}: possibilities:
- (m0,m1,m2,m3) = (0,0,0,0): zero.
- (0x80,0,0,0x80): because m3=m2=0, m1=m0=0x80? Wait condition m3=m2 and m1=m0. Let me list: choices per pair: (m1=m0) ∈ {0,0x80}, (m3=m2) ∈ {0,0x80}. 
  - (0,0): Δy2=0; Δy3=0 (m3=0); Δy0=0 (m0=0). Deterministic but trivial (zero diff) — mask 0 gives F(x)⊕F(x)=0 trivially.
  - (0x80,0): m0=m1=0x80, m2=m3=0. Δy2=0; Δy3=0; Δy0: x0 flipped by 0x80 → y0 ^= 0x02. So output diff = 0x02 (byte0). Deterministic? Let me check: y0 = rol2[x0 + y1 + 1]; x0 ^ 0x80 flips MSB, so sum changes by flipping MSB without carry... wait adding 0x80 to x0: (x0 ^ 0x80) + y1 + 1 mod 256 = (x0 + y1 + 1 + 0x80) mod 256 = flips MSB (since adding 0x80 mod 256 flips bit7). Yes deterministic. So mask 0x80800000? Wait bytes: m0=0x80 (byte0 LSB), m1=0x80 (byte1), m2=0, m3=0. So mask = 0x00008080 (byte2=0, byte3=0). Output diff = byte0 0x02 = 0x00000002.
  - (0, 0x80): m0=m1=0, m2=m3=0x80. Then Δy2=0 (u,v unchanged); Δy3 from m3=0x80 → y3 ^= 0x02; Δy0=0 (m0=0, y1 unchanged). Output diff = byte3 0x02 = 0x02000000. Mask = 0x80800000? bytes: m0=0, m1=0, m2=0x80, m3=0x80 → value = 0x80800000 (since byte3=m3<<24=0x80000000, byte2=m2<<16=0x00800000). Output diff = 0x02000000.
  - (0x80, 0x80): the 0x80808080 mask, output diff 0x02000002.

So there are three exact nontrivial differentials:
- mask 0x00008080 → output 0x00000002 (flips byte0=0x80, byte1=0x80; changes byte0 output by 0x02)
- mask 0x80800000 → output 0x02000000 (flips byte2, byte3; changes byte3 output by 0x02)
- mask 0x80808080 → output 0x02000002

Let me verify these empirically. Also maybe masks with all bytes {0x00,0x80} but not satisfying m1=m0 and m3=m2 won't be deterministic.

Let me test these three masks empirically.

If we have multiple exact differentials (masks), we can build a linear system for key bits. This is the FEAL linear attack. The linear attack recovers the round keys' high-bit structure.

1. Choose an "input pair" that yields a known ciphertext difference.
2. Derive equations for the *unknown* key XOR values at each half-byte.

Let me derive the linearization.

a = 0x00008080 (input diff for bytes 0,1)
b = 0x00000002 (output diff byte 0)
c = 0x80800000 (input diff bytes 2,3)
d = 0x02000000 (output diff byte 3)

Actually, let me look at the actual structure of the linear attack on FEAL (I'll reconstruct).

The linear attack on FEAL-4 (by Biham & Shamir?) proceeds by using the relation:

D_i = F(α) ⊕ F(α ⊕ Δ) = const

This means that the function F, restricted to the equivalence classes under XOR Δ, is "linear" (affine). In particular, the *XOR of F values* over each class is constant.

One can define a "collapsed" differential attack: since the differential Δ → D is exact, differences propagate linearly through the cipher. This yields relationships between the differences of plaintexts and ciphertexts. These relationships involve the key bits "crossing out". What does that give? Since the keys cancel in differences, it gives known linear relations between plaintext differences and ciphertext differences for *all* keys. So it doesn't recover keys directly; it just validates the cipher structure.

But the FEAL linear attack goes further: it uses chosen plaintexts to compute the *input to F at the last round* from ciphertext? Hmm.

Let me look at it from the decryption side with the exact differential:

From a ciphertext C, we can compute:
R3 = CR ⊕ CL (known)
R4 = CL

If we know K3, we can compute R2 = R4 ⊕ F(R3 ⊕ K3). If we don't, R2 is unknown.

Now, suppose we have two ciphertexts C, C' that decrypt to plaintexts with certain differences... 

Actually, let me look at the FEAL linear attack from Wikipedia's description in my memory: "The linear attack on FEAL" uses the linear approximation of the round function F to derive a set of linear equations for the expanded keys. The round function F of FEAL-8 satisfies F(α) = F(α ⊕ 0x80800000) ⊕ 0x02000000 etc. This enables expressing the last round's F output as a linear function of the round key when the input to F has the top bits zeroed.

Let me consider peeling off one round using the differential property, to recover K3.

From ciphertext: R3 = CR ⊕ CL, R4 = CL.

If we could know R2, then K3 would be recoverable from R2 = R4 ⊕ F(R3 ⊕ K3)? Wait, given R2, R4, R3, we need to find K3 such that F(R3 ⊕ K3) = R4 ⊕ R2. But R3 and R2 are both unknownish... Actually if we know R2 and R4, then we know the output of F in round 4 must be R4 ⊕ R2. And the input to F is R3 ⊕ K3. Since R3 is known, we need to find K3 such that F(R3 ⊕ K3) = known_output. The F function is a bijection? Let me check: F maps 32-bit to 32-bit. Is it a permutation? FEAL's F function is not a permutation? Let me check. If F is a bijection, then K3 is uniquely determined: K3 = F^{-1}(R4 ⊕ R2) ⊕ R3. But is F a bijection? Let me check empirically.

Actually let me check if F is injective/surjective. It might be a permutation since each byte transformation uses rol2 (bijection) and additions... but squeak with chaining: y2 from (x3^x2, x1^x0); y3 from (x3, y2); y1 from (x1^x0, y2); y0 from (x0, y1). It's a triangular y structure: given output y, can we invert step by step? y2 is known from output; then y3 gives x3 via g_function? g is a bijection in its inputs? g(a,b,x) = rol2[(a+b+x) mod 256]. Given y2 and y3, and equation y3 = g(x3, y2, 0): for a fixed b=y2, g(a, b, 0) = rol2[(a+b) mod 256] is a permutation in a (since adding constant then rol2 is bijective). So x3 = inverse_rol2(y3) - y2 mod 256. Then from y1 = g(v, y2, 0) where v = x1^x0: v = inv_rol2(y1) - y2 mod 256. And x1^x0 = v. From y2 = g(u, v, 1) where u = x3^x2: u = inv_rol2(y2) - v - 1 mod 256. Then x2 = x3 ^ u. From y0 = g(x0, y1, 1): x0 = inv_rol2(y0) - y1 - 1 mod 256. Then x1 = x0 ^ v. So yes, F is a bijection (invertible)! Good.

So F is a permutation of 32-bit words. This is important: given an F input, output is unique and invertible.

So if we know R2 (and R4), then K3 = F^{-1}(R4 ⊕ R2) ⊕ R3. Still need R2.

Similarly, from the forward direction, K0 could be recovered if we knew R1: K0 = F^{-1}(L0 ⊕ R1) ⊕ A where A=R0⊕L0.

So the challenge reduces to finding intermediate states.

Alternatively, backward with K3 only: compute R2 = R4 ^ F(R3^K3). 2^20 candidates. But R2 is the L3 state? Actually R2 after round 2 = left after round 3 (L3=R2). Can we match R2 with forward states?

Let's see: Forward: after round 3, we have (L3, R3) where L3 = R2. Hmm, L3 = R2. And R3 = ... The forward computation of R2 requires K0,K1,K2. So matching R2 forward needs 3 keys.

Let me think about the linear attack method again — it likely isn't MITM brute force but rather solving linear equations for keys.

Here's the classic "linear cryptanalysis" trick for FEAL: Because F's differential is exact, we can compute the XOR of certain internal halves *from plaintext and ciphertext alone* (without keys), by choosing specially crafted plaintexts. Then use these to solve for round key nibbles.

Let me actually look at this more concretely. Let me search memory: The FEAL linear attack (by Biham & Shamir) 

They used the following linear approximation (probability 1):
F(α) ⊕ F(α ⊕ 0x80800000) = 0x02000000
and F(α) ⊕ F(α ⊕ 0x00008080) = 0x00000002.

Then they derived:
Given plaintext (L0, R0) and ciphertext (CL, CR), and their variants, they could compute the value S = ... involving the key XORs.

Specifically, I recall the "linear attack" computes quantities like:
R3 = CR ⊕ CL, and then something like
F(R2 ⊕ K2) = ... using a linear relation to eliminate K2.

Let me try to derive a system.

Alternative straightforward approach given constraints: brute-force each 20-bit key in sequence with gradual filtering, using 32 pairs pairs. Is 4 × 2^20 = 4M f-evals feasible? Yes easily in C. But the question is how to check a key candidate independently.

Let me think about whether K3 can be checked independently given only the pairs.

For a candidate K3 (2^20), we can compute for each pair: R2 = R4 ^ F(R3 ^ K3). But also R2 = L3 (left half after round 3). No independent check without other keys.

However, maybe we can derive a condition involving only K3 by considering *differences* between two plaintexts and their ciphertexts. The differential property might give relations between intermediate states that hold regardless of other keys, involving the difference of R2 across two chosen pairs.

Let me explore the "linear attack" approach formally.

We have exact differentials for F. Let me denote the input mask and output mask pairs:
A = 0x00008080 (flip bytes 0,1 top bit); D_A = 0x00000002 (output byte 0 bit1)
B = 0x80800000 (flip bytes 2,3 top bit); D_B = 0x02000000 (output byte 3 bit1)

Consider two plaintexts P and P' = P ⊕ (Δ0). The differences propagate through the Feistel. If at each round the F inputs differ by A or B (or zero), the output differences are known constants. This lets us compute ciphertext differences as linear functions of plaintext differences, independent of keys.

For a fixed round key K, if the round input difference is A, then:
F((R⊕K) ⊕ A) ⊕ F(R⊕K) = D_A regardless of R and K.

So the round output difference for input difference A is D_A.

Now the round function: 
L' = R; R' = L ⊕ F(R ⊕ K).

If R differs by A (and L by 0 say), then:
L' differs by A
R' differs by 0 ⊕ D_A = D_A.
So (A, 0) → (A, D_A) through a round. Hmm wait: L' = R, so ΔL' = ΔR = A; R' = L ⊕ F(...), so ΔR' = ΔL ⊕ ΔF = 0 ⊕ D_A = D_A.

So a round maps difference (ΔL, ΔR) = (0, A) to (ΔL', ΔR') = (A, D_A).

Similarly for B: (0, B) → (B, D_B).

So differences propagate deterministically. This defines a linear system over these "mask" variables really. If plaintext difference is chosen to be (0, A) (left unchanged, right A), then after pre-mixing right ^= left: A' = R0 ⊕ L0; with ΔL=0, ΔR=A, ΔA = A. Round 1 F input diff = ΔA = A → output diff D_A. So the states:
Start (L0,R0) diff (0,A) → after pre-mix: (L0, A') diff (0, A).
Round1: (L1,R1) diff (A, D_A)   [L1=A', R1=L0⊕F(...) → ΔR1=0⊕D_A]
Round2: input F diff = ΔR1 = D_A. But D_A = 0x00000002 is not a known-exact-differential mask! Only A and B (and their combination) are. D_A = 2 is not a "good" mask alert. So the propagation stops being deterministic at round 2.

Hmm, so the simple differential doesn't carry through all 4 rounds with the exact masks, because the round outputs produce differences that are small (0x02), not in the exact set.

But that's okay — the linear attack only needs some rounds. Let me recall: in FEAL linear attack, they use the differential that carries with probability 1 over ALL rounds? But the intermediate differences may not stay in the exact set... Actually the historical FEAL linear attack used a *probabilistic* linear approximation that works over all rounds with high probability (like 1/2 per round), accumulating many pairs to get statistical equations.

The exact relations:
F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x00008080) = 0x00000002 for all x.   (E1)
F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x80800000) = 0x02000000 for all x.    (E2)

Consider the cipher as a whole and evaluate it on two "related" plaintexts. Choose plaintexts P and P' differing appropriately so that through rounds, the F inputs differ successively by masks from the set {0x00008080, 0x80800000}. Because these are exact, the *ciphertext difference* is a deterministic function of the plaintext difference (independent of key). That gives us a known characteristic, letting us predict ciphertext differences.

Actually here's a cleaner idea: Use the exact differential to *compute intermediate state* from both sides with fewer unknown keys.

Hmm, let me try a concrete differential characteristic using the exact masks.

We have masks A = 0x00008080 (input diff) → D_A = 0x00000002 (output diff), and B = 0x80800000 → D_B = 0x02000000.

Let me compute the propagation of a pair of plaintexts through the rounds under these exact differentials, choosing initial differences so that each round's F input difference is either A or B (where output difference is known).

Round transition (Feistel): given state diff (ΔL, ΔR) and F input diff δ = ΔR (since key cancels), output diff = φ(δ) where φ(A)=D_A, φ(B)=D_B, φ(0)=0)Skip. Then:
ΔL' = ΔR
ΔR' = ΔL ⊕ φ(ΔR)

Pre-mixing: R' = R ⊕ L → ΔA_R = ΔR ⊕ ΔL for the value entering round 1's F. Actually for round 1, F input = (R0'⊕K0) where R0' = R0⊕L0. Difference at F input = ΔR0' = ΔR0 ⊕ ΔL0.

So we can choose ΔL0, ΔR0 to control the F input differences per round.

Let me choose plaintext diff so that round 1 input diff = A: ΔR0' = ΔR0 ⊕ ΔL0 = A.

Round 1: (ΔL1, ΔR1) = (ΔR0', ΔL0 ⊕ φ(A)) = (A, ΔL0 ⊕ D_A).

Round 2 input diff = ΔR1 = ΔL0 ⊕ D_A. For known propagation, choose this to be A or B (or 0). D_A = 0x00000002, so ΔL0 must be A⊕D_A = 0x00008082 or B⊕D_A etc. Let's pick ΔL0 = A ⊕ D_A = 0x00008082. Then ΔR1 = A; round 2 input diff = A → φ = D_A.

Round 2: (ΔL2, ΔR2) = (ΔR1, ΔL1 ⊕ φ(A)) = (A, A ⊕ D_A).

Round 3 input diff = ΔR2 = A ⊕ D_A = 0x00008082. This is NOT in {0, A, B} (0x00008082 has bit1 set in byte0, not top bit... it's 0x00008080 | 0x00000002). Not an exact mask. So the characteristic breaks again.

Unless... we have exactness for masks that are combinations? Let me check: is 0x00008082 an exact mask? Probably not, but let me test. Actually, maybe it IS exact? Let me test the mask 0x00008082 empirically. It's A with an extra bit1 in byte0. Hmm.

Wait — maybe there are exact differentials for a larger set of masks. Let me test masks of the form 0x00800080, 0x00008082, 0x80800002, etc. Let me search more broadly: masks where each byte is either 0x80 or 0x82 or combinations? The exactness came from flipping top bits that cancel in XORs. Generalizing: Flipping bit 7 of bytes 0,1 (and not 2,3) affects only y0 and y2? Let me redo the byte-level analysis for a general mask.

Let me define for each byte j, input flip mask m_j (8 bits). We want ΔF output to be deterministic (independent of carries) for all inputs. 

The output bytes:
y2 = rol2[(x3⊕x2) + (x1⊕x0) + 1]
y3 = rol2[x3 + y2]
y1 = rol2[(x1⊕x0) + y2]
y0 = rol2[x0 + y1 + 1]

For the output difference to be a constant independent of x, each addition's output difference must be constant. For an 8-bit addition a + b + c mod 256, the change in value when toggling a to a⊕m (i.e., adding m' where m' is the effect of XOR mask on the value) is constant iff the change is "add without carries" for all a. The only XOR masks m such that (a XOR m) - a mod 256 is constant for all a are m = 0x00 and m = 0x80 (since adding 0x80 toggles top bit, and other bits cause carries).

But in the additions, the "value" being added isn't simply XORed by m; e.g., u = x3⊕x2 changes by m3⊕m2 when x3,x2 are XORed by masks (the XOR diff of u is m3⊕m2), but the *addition* of u to v may carry. For the sum (u+v+1), toggling u by Δu (XOR diff) changes the sum by a constant iff Δu ∈ {0, 0x80}. Similarly for v.

So constraints:
- In sum for y2: inputs u = x3⊕x2 and v = x1⊕x0. Δu = m3⊕m2, Δv = m1⊕m0. Need Δu, Δv ∈ {0, 0x80}.
- In sum for y3: x3 + y2. Δx3 = m3, Δy2 = output diff of y2. Need m3 ∈ {0,0x80} and Δy2 ∈ {0,0x80}.
- In sum for y1: v + y2. Need Δv ∈ {0,0x80} (already) and Δy2 ∈ {0,0x80}.
- In sum for y0: x0 + y1. Need m0 ∈ {0,0x80} and Δy1 ∈ {0,0x80}.

Also, y2 = rol2[...], and since rol2 is linear, if the sum's change is Δs ∈ {0, 0x80}, then Δy2 = rol2(Δs). rol2(0)=0; rol2(0x80)=0x02. So Δy2 ∈ {0, 0x02}.

For Δy3, need Δy2 ∈ {0,0x80} but it's {0,0x02} — so for y3's sum, Δy2 must be 0 (since 0x02 ∉ {0,0x80}). Therefore Δy2 = 0, meaning the sum in y2 must be unchanged: Δu = Δv = 0. Since Δy2 = rol2(Δu XOR effects)... wait the sum change isn't simply XOR; if Δu,Δv ∈ {0,0x80} and one is 0x80, the sum changes by 0x80 (toggle top bit), making Δy2 = 0x02 ≠ 0, which would force Δy3 indeterminate. Unless m3 = 0 (so y3's first input unchanged) and the contribution from Δy2 is zero. So we need Δy2 = 0, i.e., Δu = Δv = 0.

Similarly for y0, Δy1 must be 0 (since Δy2=0 ⇒ y1 unchanged) so m0 ∈ {0, 0x80}.

So constraints: Δu = m3⊕m2 = 0 and Δv = m1⊕m0 = 0, so m3=m2, m1=m0, all bytes in {0,0x80}. Exactly the masks we found (0, 0x00008080, 0x80800000, 0x80808080) with outputs 0, 0x00000002, 0x02000000, 0x02000002.

So indeed only these four masks (including trivial) are exact. Good.

So the differential attack with exact masks can only use masks A=0x00008080, B=0x80800000. A characteristic through the cipher will alternate between these masks and their XORs which break exactness. Therefore the FEAL linear attack is *probabilistic*: it uses a single round's exact differential (probability 1) but the propagation over multiple rounds has some probability <1, requiring many pairs.

Hmm wait, but the historical FEAL linear attack used a *probabilistic* approximation through all rounds and many chosen plaintexts to compute statistics. Let me recall: The attack used chosen plaintexts such that differentials hold with probability 1/2 per round, and with enough pairs you get a linear equation for the key with high confidence.

Let me think about the "linear attack" recovering round keys in terms of solving equations with the F function's inverse.

Here's a classic approach for Feistel ciphers with invertible round function and known plaintext/ciphertext pairs: If we can determine the intermediate state at round boundaries from both ends with small key subsets, MITM works like:

Encryption side: P → (encrypt with K0) → S1 → (K1) → S2 → (K2) → S3 → (K3) → C.

MITM at S2: encrypt P with (K0,K1) = 2^40; decrypt C with (K2,K3) = 2^40. Too big.

But we can do a "three-subset meet": 
- Forward: P → S1 with K0 (2^20)
- Middle: S1 → S3 with K1,K2? No, S1→S2 needs K1, S2→S3 needs K2. That's 2^40.
- Backward: C → S3 with K3 (2^20).

Then match S1 and S3... no direct equation.

What about an "Eve" trick: since F is invertible, maybe we can express K_i in terms of adjacent states, and combine to reduce the search.

Let S1 = (L1, R1), S2 = (L2, R2), S3 = (L3, R3).

Relations:
R1 = L0 ⊕ F(A ⊕ K0), L1 = A.
R2 = L1 ⊕ F(R1 ⊕ K1) = A ⊕ F(L2 ⊕ K1) [since L2=R1]
R3 = L2 ⊕ F(R2 ⊕ K2)
R4 = L3 ⊕ F(R3 ⊕ K3)
L5 = L4 ⊕ R4, with L4 = R3, R4 = CL, L5 = CR.

Keys:
K0 = F^{-1}(L0 ⊕ R1) ⊕ A  (given R1)
K1 = F^{-1}(A ⊕ R2) ⊕ L2   (given R2, L2=R1)
K2 = F^{-1}(L2 ⊕ R3) ⊕ R2
K3 = F^{-1}(R2 ⊕ R4) ⊕ R3
Wait check: R4 = L3 ⊕ F(R3 ⊕ K3) where L3 = R2. So F(R3 ⊕ K3) = R2 ⊕ R4, thus R3 ⊕ K3 = F^{-1}(R2 ⊕ R4), K3 = F^{-1}(R2 ⊕ R4) ⊕ R3. ✓.

Now we have four equations linking consecutive states. This is like a path. We know L0,R0 (plaintext) and R3,R4 (ciphertext-derived: R4=CL, R3=CR⊕CL, L4=R3).

Unknowns: R1, R2 (and L1=A known, L2=R1, L3=R2 known in terms of R1,R2).

Now, we know K0 = expand(s0) etc. If we treat the *state path* as unknown and use the key-expansion constraint (keys are 20-bit seeds), this becomes a constraint on R1,R2 being consistent across 32 pairs with the same keys. That's complex.

But here's the thing: For a *single* pair, give the 32-bit R1 and R2, the keys are determined. The cipher is bijective, so any (R1,R2) consistent yields some 32-bit keys. The key space constraint: K_i must be in {expand(s): s<2^20}. This is a 20-bit constraint per key.

Given one pair, there are many 32-bit (K0..K3) values that work (any bijective state path). But the 20-bit restriction makes it special.

The "linear attack" on FEAL (I'm now fairly sure) works like this:

For the cipher, define the "linear characteristic" using the differentials:
- The relation F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x80800000) = 0x02000000 and F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x00008080) = 0x00000002 are used to derive a *linear equation* relating plaintext, ciphertextley and the *sum of round keys*:

For FEAL (from literature): The attack shows that for each plaintext/ciphertext pair, the following holds with probability 1/2 (for the first part) or so:

```
L0 ⊕ R0 ⊕ ... ⊕ CL ⊕ CR = (something involving K0⊕K1⊕K2⊕K3)
```

Actually, I recall the "linear attack" use a *linear approximation* of the whole F function: they replace F(α) by L(α) where L is the linear map given by the "linear approximation", and this holds with probability 1/2. Over all pairs, you get equations that hold with probability biased toward 1/2+something, and with enough pairs you detect the correct key XOR.

Let me look at the linear approximation of F more concretely.

Since rol2 is linear (byte-wise rotation) and addition ≈ XOR when no carries, we have the "linear approximation":

F(x) ≈ L(x) ⊕ c

where L is the linear map where byte additions are replaced by XOR. Let me compute L explicitly:

Let x = (x0,x1,x2,x3) bytes (LSB-first).
u = x3⊕x2, v = x1⊕x0.
y2 = rol2[u ⊕ v ⊕ 1]
   = rol2[x3⊕x2⊕x1⊕x0 ⊕ 1]
y3 = rol2[x3 ⊕ y2]
   = rol2[x3 ⊕ rol2(x3⊕x2⊕x1⊕x0⊕1)]
y1 = rol2[v ⊕ y2]
   = rol2[x1⊕x0 ⊕ rol2(x3⊕x2⊕x1⊕x0⊕1)]
y0 = rol2[x0 ⊕ y1 ⊕ 1]
   = rol2[x0 ⊕ rol2(x1⊕x0 ⊕ rol2(x3⊕x2⊕x1⊕x0⊕1)) ⊕ 1]

So F(x) ≈ L(x) ⊕ const where L is a fixed GF(2) linear map (on 32-bit vectors, byte-wise rol2 operations). The constant is the XOR of the "1"s going through the rol2s, plus carry errors.

This linear approximation holds with some probability per input bit? Actually the probability that addition = XOR for all byte additions simultaneously might be low. But attacker's "linear attack" uses *many* pairs and only needs a bias.

Let me recall the actual "linear attack" procedure (I'm reconstructing from a memory of a CTF):

The attack recovers the round keys by solving a linear system over GF(2) in variables representing the *key bytes*. The key insight: because F has exact differentials, the intermediate difference pattern is known, so for each pair, the relation between plaintext, ciphertext, and keys is *linear* (over GF(2)) for certain bit positions. Specifically, the top bit of each byte of the F input propagates linearly.

Let me think about the algebra differently. The property F(x) ⊕ F(x⊕A) = D_A means F is *affine* on each coset of the subgroup generated by A. In particular, for every x, F(x) and F(x⊕A) are related. If we XOR the F outputs of both, we get constant. This is like saying the *sum over the 2-coset* is constant.

Now, consider the entire cipher. If we take a plaintext P and encrypt, and also P⊕A' where A' is chosen so that through the cipher the differences stay in the subgroups generated by A and B, then the *difference* of ciphertexts is key-independent. But more importantly, we can combine pairs to isolate key terms.

Let me recall the linear approximation of FEAL F.

The function F is made of byte-wise operations. The linear approximation substitutes addition with XOR. Over the whole F, this gives a linear map L (as I derived) such that F(x) ≈ L(x) ⊕ c. Let me compute the empirical probability that the linear approximation holds bitwise. Let me first compute L and c.

Let me derive the byte-wise linear map L over GF(2) for the F function where '+' is replaced by '^'.

Given input bytes x0,x1,x2,x3 (LSB-first), output bytes y0,y1,y2,y3:

a = x3^x2^x1^x0 (a single byte)
y2 = rol2(a ^ 1)
b = rol2(a ^ 1)   # y2
y3 = rol2(x3 ^ b)
y1 = rol2(x1^x0 ^ b)
y0 = rol2(x0 ^ y1 ^ 1)

Over GF(2), rol2 is a linear operation on 8-bit vectors (multiplication by the rotation matrix). The constants 1 appear in y2 (^1) and y0 (^1).

So L(x) = the XOR accumulation: 
y2 = rol2(x3^x2^x1^x0)
y3 = rol2(x3 ^ rol2(x3^x2^x1^x0))
y1 = rol2(x1^x0 ^ rol2(x3^x2^x1^x0))
y0 = rol2(x0 ^ y1)
and constant c: y2 gets ^1 → after rol2... wait the constant "1" inside rol2: rol2(v ^ 1) = rol2(v) ^ rol2(1) = rol2(v) ^ 0x04 (since rol2(1) = (1<<2)=4). So y2 constant = 0x04. y3 has no constant (adds 0). y1 no constant. y0's input has x0 ^ y1 ^ 1, so constant 0x01 → rol2(0x01) = 0x04. So c = byte2? Wait output assembly: y0 is byte 0, y1 byte1, y2 byte2, y3 byte3. Constants: y2 gets 0x04, y0 gets 0x04. In output, y2<<16 (0x00040000) and y0 (0x00000004). So c = 0x00040004.

Let me define L as the linear map with:
y2' = rol2(x3^x2^x1^x0)
y3' = rol2(x3 ^ y2')
y1' = rol2(x1^x0 ^ y2')
y0' = rol2(x0 ^ y1')
and F(x) = L(x) ^ 0x00040004 when the addition behaves like XOR (no carries).

Let me think about the actual mechanics of using exact differentials to recover a round key via "linear attack".

Consider the last round. We know from ciphertext: R4=CL, R3=CR⊕CL.

Round 4: R4 = L3 ⊕ F(R3 ⊕ K3), where L3 = R2 (state after round 3's left).

So F(R3 ⊕ K3) = L3 ⊕ R4 = R2 ⊕ R4.

We want K3. If we knew R2, we'd get it. R2 is the left half entering round 3, i.e., the output of round 2. R2 is unknown without K1,K2.

Now, the *linear attack* trick: use two plaintexts whose R2 values differ by a known mask, so that F(R3⊕K3) differences are known, enabling solving for K3 bits.

**FEAL-8 Linear Cryptanalysis (Biham & Shamir 1991):**

The attack uses the following exact relations (with probability 1):
- F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x80800000) = 0x02000000
- F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x00008080) = 0x00000002

They define "linear characteristics" by treating the cipher as a linear transformation over GF(2)^64 with respect to the input masks. Then they derive, for each of a set of chosen plaintexts, equations.

Actually, I found a clearer memory: The FEAL linear attack is described in the paper "Linear Cryptanalysis and FEAL" or in the book "Applied Cryptography". The gist:

1. Encrypt 8 chosen plaintexts P_0..P_7 that are all related by XORs of specific masks.
2. The corresponding ciphertexts satisfy linear equations (with the "carry" errors).
3. From these, compute the "intermediate value" after 2 rounds (the value R2 for each), 
4. Then, since R2 and R3 (from ciphertext) are known, you can derive K2 and K3 from the round equations:
   F(R2 ⊕ K2) = R1 ⊕ R3, and F(R3 ⊕ K3) = R2 ⊕ R4.
   With enough equations, solve for K2, K3 byte-by-byte (each byte 2^8 or 2^16 by partial search).
5. Then peel off rounds 2-4 and recover K0, K1 similarly using the plaintexts.

The key trick is #3: computing R2 without knowing K1,K2,K3, using the chosen plaintext relations and the exact differentials.

How to compute R2 from chosen plaintexts? Let me derive.

For a pair of plaintexts P and P* such that the ciphertexts satisfy certain relations, the values of R2 can be deduced via the linear differential structure.

Actually here's a cleaner idea to get R2 without keys, using the exact differential, for *any single pair* (not chosen):

From ciphertext: R3 = CR⊕CL, R4 = CL.

From round 3: R2 = L3 = ... also L3 = R2; and R3 = L2 ⊕ F(R2⊕K2) where L2 = R1.
From round 2: R2 = A ⊕ F(R1⊕K1).

Nothing gives R2 without keys.

BUT: with S3 known for 32 pairsбоислэт, we can recover K0,K1,K2 by a MITM: 
- Forward from P with K0 to get S1.
- Backward from S3 with K2 to get S2 (since R3 known, and L2 = R3 ⊕ F(R2⊕K2); wait backward from S3: we know L3=R2, R3; to compute S2 we need K2: R3 = L2 ⊕ F(R2⊕K2) → L2 = R3 ⊕ F(L3⊕K2). So given K2, we compute L2 from S3. And S2 = (L2, R2) = (L2, L3). 
- Then match S2 with forward: forward S2 = (L2, R2) requires K0,K1. That's 40 bits forward. Not MITM since forward needs 2 keys and backward needs 1.

3-round split: P --K0--> S1 --K1--> S2 --K2--> S3. Backward from S3 with K2 gives S2 (2^20). Forward from P with K0 gives S1 (2^20). Matching at S2 requires K1 on forward side (since S1→S2 needs K1). So:
- Forward: P →(K0)→ S1 →(K1)→ S2. (K0,K1) = 2^40.
- Backward: S3 →(K2)→ S2. (K2) = 2^20.

Maybe the "linear attack" here refers to the following well-known technique for FEAL: **Because the difference F(x)⊕F(x⊕0x80800000)=0x02000000 is exact, the cipher can be reduced to a LINEAR cipher (over the XOR of 0x80800000) with 4 "linear" equations that let you recover the round keys one byte at a time.**

Let me try to find the actual FEAL linear attack equations by searching my memory harder.

I recall now: The attack is described in the paper **"Linear and Differential Cryptanalysis"** and it works like this for FEAL-4:

1. Define the *linear transformation* T on 64-bit blocks: T(A,B) = ... constructed from the F function's exact differentials. Since F has these exact linear relationships, T maps (plaintext XOR mask) to (ciphertext XOR mask) linearly.

2. The *linear attack*: pick a plaintext Pl and a set of derived plaintexts Pl ⊕ Δ for various Δ (the "linear" masks), encrypt all, and use the *ciphertext differences*, which are known, to compute the *difference* of intermediate states. Then, since you know the difference of the F input at a certain round, and the difference depends only on key XORs (which cancel), you solve for the key.

Let me think about an entirely different approach that is likely the intended and feasible one:

**Linear attack = solve for the keys using linear algebra over GF(2), treating the F function outputs as unknown variables that we compute from the pairs via the cipher equations, then use the 20-bit seed constraint.**

Let me formalize. We have 32 pairs. For each pair j, define:
- A_j = R0_j ⊕ L0_j (known)
- R1_j = L0_j ⊕ F(A_j ⊕ K0)
- R2_j = A_j ⊕ F(R1_j ⊕ K1)
- R3_j = CL_j ⊕ CR_j (known from ciphertext!)
- R4_j = CL_j (known)
- R2_j = R4_j ⊕ F(R3_j ⊕ K3)  [from round 4]
- R3_j = R1_j ⊕ F(R2_j ⊕ K2)  [from round 3]

So we have unknowns: K0,K1,K2,K3 (four 32-bit keys, but each in {expand(s): s<2^20}) and for each pair the intermediate R1_j, R2_j.

Equations per pair:
(1) R1_j = L0_j ⊕ F(A_j ⊕ K0)
(2) R2_j = A_j ⊕ F(R1_j ⊕ K1)
(3) R3_j = R1_j ⊕ F(R2_j ⊕ K2)
(4) R2_j = R4_j ⊕ F(R3_j ⊕ K3)

All 4 equations must hold.

If we guess K0 (2^20), we compute R1_j per pair. Then equation (3) and (4) give:
R2_j from (4): R2_j = R4_j ⊕ F(R3_j ⊕ K3). Depends on K3.
R2_j from (2): R2_j = A_j ⊕ F(R1_j ⊕ K1). Depends on K1.
And (3): R3_j = R1_j ⊕ F(R2_j ⊕ K2). Depends on K2.

So if we guess K0, we know R1_j. Then we have unknown K1,K2,K3 and R2_j.

Now, equation (3): F(R2_j ⊕ K2) = R1_j ⊕ R3_j (known, since R1_j known from K0 and R3_j known from C!). This is a relation between R2_j and K2. Since F is invertible:
R2_j ⊕ K2 = F^{-1}(R1_j ⊕ R3_j) =: M_j (computable for each pair once K0 fixed!)
So R2_j = M_j ⊕ K2.   (*)

Beautiful: once K0 is fixed, R2_j is expressed in terms of the unknown K2. Let me verify: (3) says R3 = L2 ⊕ F(R2⊕K2) where L2 = R1. So indeed F(R2⊕K2) = R1 ⊕ R3. Thus R2⊕K2 = F^{-1}(R1⊕R3), so R2 = F^{-1}(R1⊕R3) ⊕ K2. Yes!

Similarly, equation (4): R2_j = R4_j ⊕ F(R3_j ⊕ K3) gives a relation between K3 and R2_j.

And equation (2): R2_j = A_j ⊕ F(R1_j ⊕ K1) gives K1.

Now, combining (*) with (4):
R2_j = R4_j ⊕ F(R3_j ⊕ K3) AND R2_j = M_j ⊕ K2
⇒ M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ R4_j = F(R3_j ⊕ K3).

So we have for each pair j:
F(R3_j ⊕ K3) = M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ R4_j.    (Eq A)

This relates K2 and K3 through the known pair data (M_j, R3_j, R4_j all known once K0 fixed, except M_j which needs F^{-1}).

Now combine (*) with (2):
R2_j = A_j ⊕ F(R1_j ⊕ K1) AND R2_j = M_j ⊕ K2
⇒ A_j ⊕ F(R1_j ⊕ K1) = M_j ⊕ K2
⇒ F(R1_j ⊕ K1) = M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ A_j.   (Eq B)

Also R1_j = L0_j ⊕ F(A_j ⊕ K0), known.

So we have the system with K1,K2,K3:

For each pair j:
(B) F(R1_j ⊕ K1) = M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ A_j
(A) F(R3_j ⊕ K3) = M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ R4_j

where M_j = F^{-1}(R1_j ⊕ R3_j).

Now K1, K2, K3 are unknown 32-bit keys (with 20-bit seed structure: each K_i = expand(s_i)).

We have 2 equations per pair involving 3 key unknowns. Total 64 equations (32 pairs × 2), 96 key unknowns bits (3×32), but with the 20-bit structure 60 bits. Hmm, but these equations involve F (nonlinear in K1 and K3). They're twisted: for each pair, given a candidate K2, we can derive K1 and K3 via inverse images: 

(B) implies F(R1_j ⊕ K1) = Bval_j := M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ A_j, so K1 = F^{-1}(Bval_j) ⊕ R1_j — but this must be CONSISTENT for all pairs! For the true K2, all 32 pairs must give the same K1 (the actual key). Similarly (A): K3 = F^{-1}(Aval_j) ⊕ R3_j with Aval_j := M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ R4_j, must be consistent across all pairs.

So: If we fix K0 and K2, then K1 and K3 are determined (and consistency checkable) via:
K1_candidate_j = F^{-1}(M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ A_j) ⊕ R1_j   for each pair j
K3_candidate_j = F^{-1}(M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ R4_j) ⊕ R3_j   for each pair j

If all 32 pairs yield the same K1 value for a given (K0, K2) and same K3 value, then we have found the keys!

Wait, but we also need to check equation (4) — we used it. And the R2 expression. Let me make sure all equations are captured.

Let me recount the full system. For fixed K0:
- R1_j = L0_j ⊕ F(A_j ⊕ K0) [eq 1 used]
- M_j = F^{-1}(R1_j ⊕ R3_j) [from eq 3]
- Eq B: F(R1_j ⊕ K1) = M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ A_j [eq 2]
- Eq A: F(R3_j ⊕ K3) = M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ R4_j [eq 4]

So given K0 and K2, we can compute candidate K1 and K3 from each pair. Consistency across pairs gives the solution. 

The search: K0 (2^20) × K2 (2^20) = 2^40. Too much? 2^40 is 1T; maybe optimized C with early abort could be feasible? Each consistency check involves 32 F-inverse evaluations. 1T × 32 is way too slow.

But wait — we can use MITM! The equations separate:

Eq B determines K1 from (K0, K2) — but actually Eq B gives K1 via K2 per pair (with K0 giving R1 and M). Eq A gives K3 from (K0, K2) via same K2. Since K1 and K3 determinations both depend on K0 and K2, we can't fully separate K0 and K2. However, we can do a MITM as:

For each (K0), compute for all pairs the values R1_j, M_j.
For each K2, the candidate K1_j = F^{-1}(M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ A_j) ⊕ R1_j must be the same across j. This is a *check* that doesn't involve K3. Similarly the K3 consistency is independent: K3_j = F^{-1}(M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ R4_j) ⊕ R3_j must be same across j.

These are two separate tests, both depending on (K0,K2). But here's the trick: we can solve for K2 given K0 *alone* using the structure!

Actually, from Eq B: K1 = F^{-1}(M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ A_j) ⊕ R1_j must be constant over j. Equivalently, for pairs j and k:
F^{-1}(M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ A_j) ⊕ R1_j = F^{-1}(M_k ⊕ K2 ⊕ A_k) ⊕ R1_k.

Two equations (for different j,k) in the single unknown K2 (32 bits, but only 2^20 seed space!). So with K0 fixed, we can solve for K2 using 2 pairs! Then K1 is determined. Similarly Eq A gives K3.

So the algorithm:
1. Fix K0 (2^20 candidates).
2. For each candidate K0, compute R1_j and M_j for all 32 pairs.
3. Solve for K2 using two pairs: search over 2^20 seeds for the seed s2 such that expand(s2) satisfies the pair-consistency equation. This is 2^20 per K0 → 2^40 total. Still too much!

Actually, let me revisit the FEAL linear attack literature. Let me try to recall the *explicit* steps of the linear attack on FEAL-4 with 32-bit keys.

I'll reconstruct from the mathematical structure of exact differentials. I think the attack works as follows:

Because F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x80800000) = 0x02000000 exactly for all x, the *XOR of the top halves* behaves linearly. Specifically, consider the *XOR-sum* of F over all inputs varying in the top-bit positions... 

Let me define a reduced linear transformation. For a 32-bit input x, write x = (x_hi, x_lo) where x_hi = top bit mask (bit7 of each byte) and x_lo = rest. Hmm.

Consider the equivalence class of inputs under XOR of 0x80800000 and 0x00008080: these generate a subgroup G of size 4: {0, A, B, A⊕B}. The exact differentials say F is 'affine' on each coset of this subgroup... but on each coset (size 4), the XOR of all 4 F values? Let's compute: For a coset {x, x⊕A, x⊕B, x⊕A⊕B}, the F values satisfy:
F(x)⊕F(x⊕A)=0x00000002
F(x)⊕F(x⊕B)=0x02000000
F(x)⊕F(x⊕A⊕B)=0x02000002 (=0x00000002⊕0x02000000)
Consistent (the last relation is the XOR of the first two, as A⊕B = combined mask). So the four F-values form a structure: F(x⊕A) = F(x)⊕0x00000002, F(x⊕B)=F(x)⊕0x02000000, F(x⊕A⊕B)=F(x)⊕0x02000002.

So on each coset, F acts like a linear map: the output differences are the XOR of the input differences mapped by a linear "top-bit" map. Specifically, the mask A (0x00008080) maps to output 0x00000002, mask B (0x80800000) maps to 0x02000000. This restricted map is linear (it's just the identity-like map on the 2-dimensional subspace).

This means: if the round keys are also considered modulo this subgroup, and if the plaintexts are chosen so that the round-F inputs stay within a coset, the cipher behaves *linearly* on the quotient. The attack uses this to *compute the difference* (coset) information through the cipher from plaintext differences and ciphertext differences, which are observable.

But again, the given pairs are random; their differences won't naturally be in the subgroup. However, we can *combine* pairs linearly: e.g., for any two pairs, form XOR of ciphertexts etc.

The linear attack on FEAL-8:

2. The equations are derived from the fact that for FEAL's F, certain linear relations hold with probability 1/2 (or 1), yielding linear equations that *involve key bits*.

- Since the keys are 20-bit seeds, recover seeds by checking.

Eq B: F(R1_j ⊕ K1) = M_j ⊕ K2 ⊕ A_j.
Given K0, R1_j and M_j are known for all pairs. The unknowns are K1 (32 bits but 20-bit seed) and K2 (32 bits, 20-bit seed). For each pair, the equation is one constraint. With ≥2 pairs, we can solve for K1 and K2.

Rewrite: F(R1_j ⊕ K1) ⊕ K2 = M_j ⊕ A_j =: N_j (known per pair, given K0).

So for each pair j: F(R1_j ⊕ K1) ⊕ K2 = N_j.

This is like: two unknowns (K1, K2) and for each pair, one equation. The structure: for two pairs j,k:
F(R1_j ⊕ K1) ⊕ F(R1_k ⊕ K1) = N_j ⊕ N_k.   [Eq B']

This equation involves ONLY K1 (K2 eliminated)! 

Yes! So:
F(R1_j ⊕ K1) ⊕ F(R1_k ⊕ K1) = N_j ⊕ N_k, where N_j = M_j ⊕ A_j = F^{-1}(R1_j⊕R3_j) ⊕ A_j.

Since R1_j and N_j are known given K0, this is an equation in K1 alone. With 32 pairs, we have a system to solve for K1 (2^20). Then K2 = F(R1_j⊕K1) ⊕ N_j for any j (consistency check).

Similarly, from Eq A: F(R3_j ⊕ K3) ⊕ K2 = M_j ⊕ R4_j =: P_j. Eliminating K2:
F(R3_j ⊕ K3) ⊕ F(R3_k ⊕ K3) = P_j ⊕ P_k.   [Eq A']

So given K0, we can solve K1 using pairs (Eq B') and K3 using pairs (Eq A'), each by searching 2^20! Then K2 is determined by either equation as a consistency check.

So the total work: for each K0 (2^20), solve for K1 (2^20) and K3 (2^20), and check K2. That's 2^40 again if done naively per pair of (K0, K1). Hmm, but actually we can solve K1 for each candidate K0 using a *cross-pair consistency check* that costs O(32) per K1 candidate. Let me try to search memory of the specific attack on this exact cipher (this looks like a known CTF challenge, maybe from "DownUnderCTF" or "SECCON" or "CryptoHack"?). "FEAL-like" with 20-bit key seeds and "linear attack" — this is likely a CryptoHack-style challenge. The intended solution might be:

The the FEAL F function satisfies F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x80800000) = 0x02000000. This means we can build a *linear* approximation of the cipher over GF(2) with respect to the XOR of specific masksrits. Then:

Actually, let me reconsider the differential propagation with the exact masks. There are two independent exact differentials (A→D_A, B→D_B). If we choose *plaintext pairs* that differ by a mask designed so that at every round the F input difference is either A or B, the ciphertext difference is fully deterministic and key-independent (since keys cancel). The chosen mask must make the differences at each round land in {0,A,B,A⊕B}. Let me see if such a mask exists (a "consistent characteristic").

We need to find input difference (ΔL0, ΔR0) such that all round F-input differences are in set {0, A, B} (where output diff known). Let me write the transition. Round maps (ΔL, ΔR):
ΔF_input = ΔR
Δ_F_output = φ(ΔR) where φ(A)=D_A, φ(B)=D_B, φ(0)=0.
New (ΔL', ΔR') = (ΔR, ΔL ⊕ φ(ΔR)).

So starting with (ΔL0, ΔR0'), after pre-mix ΔR0' = ΔR0 ⊕ ΔL0 is round-1 F input (mask for F). We choose this in {A,B,0}.

Let me search for a cycle/chains of masks. Represent each mask value as one of {0,A,B,A⊕B}? But φ is only defined for {0,A,B}. The transition can produce values outside the set (e.g., A⊕D_A).

From our earlier attempt: start round1 F input = A. Then (ΔL1,ΔR1) = (A, ΔL0⊕D_A). To keep ΔR1 ∈ {0,A,B}, choose ΔL0 = A⊕D_A = 0x00008082 — NOT in {0,A,B}, but ΔL0 is the plaintext left diff, which can be any value; only the F-input differences need to be in the set. So choose ΔL0 = A ⊕ D_A. Then ΔR1 = A. And ΔL1 = A (which is in set, good). Round2: F input ΔR1 = A → φ=D_A. (ΔL2,ΔR2) = (A, ΔL1⊕D_A) = (A, A⊕D_A). Now ΔR2 = A⊕D_A = 0x00008082 ∉ {0,A,B}. So the characteristic breaks at round 2's F input (0x00008082 not in set).

Could we choose the plaintext so that ΔR2 ∈ {0,A,B}? ΔR2 = ΔL1 ⊕ D_A in round 2 = A ⊕ D_A = 0x00008082 again (independent of ΔL0 since ΔL1=A). So impossible to keep all 4 rounds in the set with φ exactness. Thus a deterministic characteristic through all 4 rounds using only exact masks does NOT exist (for this round count).

Therefore the historical FEAL linear attack must use *probabilistic* propagation: even when the F input difference is not in {0,A,B}, there is a *linear approximation* that holds with probability 1/2 or so. This is why the FEAL linear attack is *statistical*: it uses chosen plaintexts and many pairs to detect the bias.

Given our problem: 32 random pairs... hmm, 32 pairs is small but maybe the attack only needs a few rounds of exact propagation (probabilistic with high bias). Let me reconsider.

Let me actually search my memory for "FEAL linear attack" algorithm:

The attack:
1. Choose a plaintext P. Compute P' = P ⊕ (0, 0x80800000) or similar.
2. Encrypt both P and P' (this is a chosen-plaintext attack). Get C, C'.
3. Because of the exact differential, C⊕C' is known (key-independent). This allows computing the difference of certain intermediate values.
4. With 4 such chosen pairs (using masks A and B at specific rounds), compute all intermediate differences at round boundaries.
5. Then, since you know intermediate *differences*, you can deduce key XOR relationships and peel rounds.

**The linear attack idea (reconstructed):**

Since we have 32 random known pairs and small per-key space, why not do a **divide-and-conquer MITM with 2^20 keys** at two independent points?

Let me look at the encryption as a composition:
C = F_K3( F_K2( F_K1( F_K0(P') ) ) ) plus post-processing.

We can invert outer rounds:

Given ciphertext C, compute the state after round 3 (S3 = (L3,R3)) if we knew K3:
R3 = CL ⊕ CR (known!)
R2 = L3 = CL ⊕ F(R3 ⊕ K3)  [since R4 = L3 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3), L3 = R4 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3)]

Wait: L3 = R4 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3) = CL ⊕ F(R3⊕K3). And R3 = CL⊕CR known. So given K3, we can compute S3 = (L3,R3) = (CL ⊕ F(R3⊕K3), CL⊕CR). Yes.

Given plaintext P, compute S1 (after round 1) if we knew K0:
A = R0 ⊕ L0
L1 = A
R1 = L0 ⊕ F(A⊕K0)

Now, S3 and S1 are connected by rounds 2 and 3 with keys K1, K2. Specifically:
S1 = (L1, R1)
S2 = (L2, R2) = (R1, L1 ⊕ F(R1⊕K1))
S3 = (L3, R3) = (R2, L2 ⊕ F(R2⊕K2))

Now given S1 and S3, we can solve for K1 and K2! For a single pair:
- R2 = L3 (known from S3).
- L2 = R1 (known from S1).
- Round 3: R3 = L2 ⊕ F(R2⊕K2) ⇒ F(L3 ⊕ K2) = R1 ⊕ R3 ⇒ K2 = F^{-1}(R1⊕R3) ⊕ L3.  [depends on R1 from K0]
- Round 2: R2 = L1 ⊕ F(R1⊕K1) ⇒ F(R1⊕K1) = L1 ⊕ R2 = L1 ⊕ L3 ⇒ K1 = F^{-1}(L1⊕L3) ⊕ R1.

So given (K0, K3), for each pair we can compute K1 and K2 directly! And the correct (K0,K3) yields consistent K1,K2 across all 32 pairs. Search space: (K0,K3) = 2^40. Hmm still 2^40.

But wait: for a single pair, (K0,K3) → (K1,K2) is a deterministic mapping. Consistency across pairs gives the solution. The search is 2^40.

K2 = F^{-1}(R1_j ⊕ R3_j) ⊕ L3_j.

- Backward with K2 (2^20): S2 = (R3⊕F(L3⊕K2), L3).
Match! This is a standard 2-round MITM: 2^20 forward values and 2^20 backward values per pair; match on the 64-bit S2.

These combine through rounds to give a linear relation between plaintext bits, ciphertext bits, and key bits holding with probability 1/2 + ε'. For each key bit, you test both 0/1 hypotheses across many plaintext/ciphertext pairs and pick the one giving higher correlation.

Let me try to set up equations in K1,K2 only, eliminating K0,K3 using cross-pair relations.

From earlier: given K0 and K3, K1 and K2 are determined. But let's eliminate K0,K3 to get equations in K1,K2.

(2) R2 = A ⊕ F(R1⊕K1)
(3) R3 = R1 ⊕ F(R2⊕K2)
(4) R2 = R4 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3)

From (1): A⊕K0 = F^{-1}(L0⊕R1) ⇒ K0 = F^{-1}(L0⊕R1) ⊕ A.
From (4): R3⊕K3 = F^{-1}(R2⊕R4) ⇒ K3 = F^{-1}(R2⊕R4) ⊕ R3.

So K0 and K3 are *determined* by the intermediate states R1,R2! And K1 from (2): K1 = F^{-1}(A⊕R2) ⊕ R1 (since R2 = A⊕F(R1⊕K1) ⇒ F(R1⊕K1)=A⊕R2 ⇒ R1⊕K1 = F^{-1}(A⊕R2) ⇒ K1 = F^{-1}(A⊕R2) ⊕ R1).
And K2 from (3): K2 = F^{-1}(R1⊕R3) ⊕ R2.

So the *entire key* is determined by (R1,R2) for a single pair:
K0 = F^{-1}(L0⊕R1) ⊕ A
K1 = F^{-1}(A⊕R2) ⊕ R1
K2 = F^{-1}(R1⊕R3) ⊕ R2
K3 = F^{-1}(R2⊕R4) ⊕ R3

where A=R0⊕L0 (plaintext), R3,R4 (ciphertext), and R1,R2 are intermediate states.

Now the *constraint* is that these four derived keys must be the same across all 32 pairs. Moreover, each derived key must be of the form expand(s) with s<2^20.

For a single pair, any (R1,R2) gives *some* 32-bit keys. The number of possible (R1,R2) is 2^64, but the key constraint reduces it to (2^20)^4 = 2^80 seed combos — which matches the unique key.

Cross-pair consistency: For pairs j and k:
K0_j = F^{-1}(L0_j⊕R1_j) ⊕ A_j must equal K0_k = F^{-1}(L0_k⊕R1_k) ⊕ A_k.
This relates R1_j and R1_k. Similarly for others.

So the attack reduces to: find the unique (R1_j, R2_j) for each pair consistent with a single key. That's like solving a big system. This is essentially brute force over 32×64 bits of state with constraints. Not helpful directly.

BUT here's a thought: maybe we can recover the keys by using only TWO pairs and solving with a MITM/SMT, since 2 pairs give enough equations. Let me think about the smallest feasible computation:

Unknowns: 4 keys (each 20-bit seed) = 80 bits.
For 2 pairs, the consistency equations give constraints. Solving 80-bit unknown with 2 pairs via MITM:

For each pair j, and each choice of (R1_j,R2_j), we derive keys. Consistency between pairs requires derived keys equal. Since (R1_j,R2_j) is 64 bits, enumerating all is 2^64 per pair — impossible.

But we can enumerate key seeds instead. Eh.

**Reconstructing the FEAL Linear Attack:**

The F function satisfies:
(1) F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x80800000) = 0x02000000  [call this property P1]
(2) F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x00008080) = 0x00000002  [property P2]

Now, consider the encryption of a plaintext P = (L0, R0) and another plaintext P' = (L0 ⊕ a, R0 ⊕ b) designed so that the difference in the *round-1 F input* is 0x80800000, then round-1 F output difference is 0x02000000, etc. By chaining these, we get differences at every round. The key insight: choose a and b so that **for rounds 1,2,3,4 the F input differences are alternately 0x80800000 and 0x00008080**, because those are the exact masks. Does such a sequence exist? Let me compute.

We need the F-input difference at each round (i.e., difference in the right half before F) to be in the exact set {A, B} where A=0x00008080, B=0x80800000.

Round r transition: (ΔL,ΔR) → (ΔL', ΔR') = (ΔR, ΔL ⊕ φ(ΔR)) where φ(B)=D_B=0x02000000, φ(A)=D_A=0x00000002.

The F-input difference at round r is ΔR (the right half before F in that round). Wait need to be careful: in round r, the input to F is R_i ⊕ K_i where R_i is the right half at the START of round r (i.e., R_{r} if rounds numbered 1..4 with R_i being right half after round i... let me use consistent notation).

Let me use: state before round 1: (L0, A) where A=R0⊕L0 (after premix). Round 1 F input = A ⊕ K0, diff = ΔA (which is ΔR0 ⊕ ΔL0).
Let δ1 = ΔA (F input diff round 1).
Round 1 output: (ΔL1, ΔR1) = (δ1, ΔL0 ⊕ φ(δ1)).  [since L1=A, R1=L0⊕F(...)]
Round 2 F input diff = ΔR1 = ΔL0 ⊕ φ(δ1) =: δ2.
Round 2 output: (ΔL2, ΔR2) = (δ2, ΔL1 ⊕ φ(δ2)) = (δ2, δ1 ⊕ φ(δ2)).
Round 3 F input diff = ΔR2 = δ1 ⊕ φ(δ2) =: δ3.
Round 3 output: (ΔL3, ΔR3) = (δ3, ΔL2 ⊕ φ(δ3)) = (δ3, δ2 ⊕ φ(δ3)).
Round 4 F input diff = ΔR3 = δ2 ⊕ φ(δ3) =: δ4.
Round 4 output: (ΔL4, ΔR4) = (δ4, ΔL3 ⊕ φ(δ4)) = (δ4, δ3 ⊕ φ(δ4)).
Post: ΔL5 = ΔL4 ⊕ ΔR4 = δ4 ⊕ (δ3 ⊕ φ(δ4)) = δ3 ⊕ δ4 ⊕ φ(δ4).
Cipher diff: ΔCL = ΔR4 = δ3 ⊕ φ(δ4), ΔCR = ΔL5 = δ3 ⊕ δ4 ⊕ φ(δ4).

We can choose δ1 = ΔL0⊕ΔR0 (through the plaintext). Then δ2 = ΔL0 ⊕ φ(δ1). δ3 = δ1 ⊕ φ(δ2). δ4 = δ2 ⊕ φ(δ3).

We want δ1,δ2,δ3,δ4 ∈ {0,A,B} with exact φ. Let me see if there's a valid assignment. φ(A)=D_A=0x00000002, φ(B)=D_B=0x02000000.

Choose δ1 = A (so φ(δ1)=D_A).
Then δ2 = ΔL0 ⊕ D_A. We want δ2 ∈ {A,B}. Set ΔL0 = A ⊕ D_A = 0x00008082 (arbitrary plaintext left diff not in the set; fine).
δ2 = A.
δ3 = δ1 ⊕ φ(δ2) = A ⊕ D_A = 0x00008082. But we want δ3 ∈ {0,A,B} for exactness. 0x00008082 is NOT in the set → φ(δ3) undefined (not exact). So we cannot have all four rounds exact with the A-mask path.

Try δ1 = B: φ(B)=D_B=0x02000000.
δ2 = ΔL0 ⊕ D_B; set ΔL0 = B ⊕ D_B = 0x80800000 ⊕ 0x02000000 = 0x82800000; then δ2 = B.
δ3 = δ1 ⊕ φ(δ2) = B ⊕ D_B = 0x82800000 ∉ {A,B}. Breaks again.

Try making them alternate:
δ1 = A. δ2: choose ΔL0 = B ⊕ D_A = 0x80800000 ⊕ 0x00000002 = 0x80800002 → δ2 = B.
δ3 = δ1 ⊕ φ(δ2) = A ⊕ D_B = 0x00008080 ⊕ 0x02000000 = 0x02008080 ∉ {A,B}. Breaks.

δ1 = B, δ2 = A: ΔL0 = A ⊕ D_B = 0x00008080 ⊕ 0x02000000 = 0x02008080.
δ3 = B ⊕ φ(A) = 0x80800000 ⊕ 0x00000002 = 0x80800002 ∉ {A,B}. Breaks.

So with only 4 rounds pending endless, full exact 4-round characteristic impossible. This confirms the historical linear attack must handle non-exact masks (probabilistically) or only use exactness partially.

Let me look at this from the MITM angle again, but with an important twist: **use the F function's invertibility to eliminate keys and reduce to 2^20 searches.**

Recall the full state equations and key derivation:
K0 = F^{-1}(L0⊕R1) ⊕ A

K2 = F^{-1}(R1_j⊕R3_j) ⊕ R2_j
K3 = F^{-1}(R2_j⊕R4_j) ⊕ R3_j

From the last two:
K2 ⊕ R2_j = F^{-1}(R1_j⊕R3_j)  ⇒ R2_j = F^{-1}(R1_j⊕R3_j) ⊕ K2.
K3 ⊕ R3_j = F^{-1}(R2_j⊕R4_j) ⇒ R2_j ⊕ R4_j = F(R3_j ⊕ K3) ⇒ R2_j = F(R3_j⊕K3) ⊕ R4_j.

Setting equal:
F^{-1}(R1_j⊕R3_j) ⊕ K2 = F(R3_j⊕K3) ⊕ R4_j
⇒ F^{-1}(R1_j⊕R3_j) ⊕ K2 ⊕ R4_j = F(R3_j⊕K3).

This is exactly Eq A from before with M_j = F^{-1}(R1_j⊕R3_j).

F^{-1}(R1_0⊕R3_0) ⊕ K2 ⊕ R4_0 = F(R3_0⊕K3)
F^{-1}(R1_1⊕R3_1) ⊕ K2 ⊕ R4_1 = F(R3_1⊕K3)

Eliminate K2: subtract (XOR) the two equations:
[F^{-1}(R1_0⊕R3_0) ⊕ R4_0] ⊕ [F^{-1}(R1_1⊕R3_1) ⊕ R4_1] = F(R3_0⊕K3) ⊕ F(R3_1⊕K3).

**Idea: use the F bijection to convert the 4-key problem into a search over ONE inner state plus consistency.**

Actually, let me revisit the "(K0,K2) search" but invert the search direction for speed. 

For each K0 (2^20), we have 32 pairs with known (R1_j, M_j, A_j, R3_j, R4_j). We need to find K2 such that K1 = F^{-1}(M_j⊕K2⊕A_j)⊕R1_j is consistent across pairs AND K3 = F^{-1}(M_j⊕K2⊕R4_j)⊕R3_j is consistent.

Instead of iterating K2 for each K0 (2^40), we can build a **hash table mapping consistency**:

For each K0, we have pairs. From pair 0, for every possible K2 (2^20), compute:
V1_0 = F^{-1}(M_0⊕K2⊕A_0)⊕R1_0  ⇒ this is the K1 that pair 0 implies.
Store in a hash table: V1_0 → list of K2.
Then for pair 1, for every K2, compute V1_1 = F^{-1}(M_1⊕K2⊕A_1)⊕R1_1. Look up V1_1 in the table; if found, we have a (K2) that makes pairs 0,1 agree on K1. Then check K3 consistency (K3 derived from pair 0 and pair 1 must agree) and remaining pairs.

Per K0: 2^20 (build table) + 2^20 (lookup) = 2^21 operations. Times 2^20 K0 = 2^41. Still too slow.

Hmm. What if we do the MITM differently: fix K0 (2^20), then solve K2 by matching across pairs via a *hash on K1*:

For pair1, K1 = G_1(K2). We need G_0(K2)=G_1(K2). Those are two functions from 2^20→2^32. The chance they agree for a random K2 is ~2^20 × 2^32 / 2^32... The number of K2 that make G_0=G_1 is expected 2^20 × (2^32/2^32)?? Hmm, G_0(K2) and G_1(K2) are both 32-bit outputs; for random K2, the probability G_0(K2)=G_1(K2) is 2^-32 (if the maps are "random"). So expected number of K2 satisfying one cross-pair consistency is 2^20 × 2^-32 = 2^-12 < 1. So with just 2 pairs, the correct K2 is (with high probability) the unique K2 satisfying G_0=G_1! That means for each K0, we can find K2 by searching 2^20 with a cross-pair check exploiting a hash table lookup: build table of G_0(K2)→K2 (size 2^20), then for each K2, check G_1(K2) in table.

**Key realization attempt:** Maybe we can recover K0 and K2 jointly using a hash table on a *combined* variable that avoids 2^40. Let's define:

For each pair j and candidate K0, define W_j(K0) = (R1_j, M_j). For each K2, the implied K1 is H_j(K0,K2) = F^{-1}(M_j⊕K2⊕A_j)⊕R1_j Believe.

We want H_0(K0,K2)=H_1(K0,K2)=... This is a constraint on (K0,K2) — 40 bits of unknown with 32×32 bits of constraint. This is essentially a 2^40 joint search. A hash MITM on K0 vs K2: 

Build table over K0: for pair 0 and K0, we need something that can be matched with K2 for pair 0... The equation H_0(K0,K2)=K1 involves both. We could parametrize by K1: for each K0 and each pair j, we can *quantify* which K2 makes K1 consistent: K2_j = M_j⊕A_j⊕F(R1_j⊕K1) (from Eq B: K2 = N_j ⊕ F(R1_j⊕K1)). So given (K0, K1), K2 is completely determined per pair! And K2 must be consistent across pairs.

So:
- Fix K0 (2^20), fix K1 (2^20) → compute K2 per pair (fast). Consistency check over 32 pairs.
Total search (K0,K1) = 2^40.

Similarly (K0,K3) = 2^40.

So all roads lead to 2^40. The price of two unknowns is inherent.

Therefore, the intended attack MUST reduce the key space below 2^40 using the linear differential structure, recovering *partial key info* (like XORs) to shrink the joint search.

**The linear attack (historical) in detail:**

I'm now recalling the actual method from the paper "Differential Cryptanalysis of FEAL" ... no wait, "linear attack" is from the same cryptanalysis family. Let me recall the key trick:

In FEAL, the F function satisfies F(α) ⊕ F(α ⊕ 0x80800000) = 0x02000000. This means the *top bit* of bytes 2,3 of the input determines bit 1 of byte 3 of the output linearly. 

The FEAL linear attack recovers the *32-bit extended keys* by the following two-step procedure:

Step 1 (finding the extended key XOR): For each plaintext P, compute the ciphertext C. Then compute:
   A = P_L ⊕ P_R ⊕ ...

From lecture notes on FEAL linear cryptanalysis:

"The linear attack on FEAL-8 uses the following fact: F(X) ⊕ F(X ⊕ 0x80800000) = 0x02000000. If we consider the 32-bit values as the XOR of two halves of the 64-bit block, the cipher can be simplified to a linear transformation when the inputs differ by 0x80800000. This gives rise to a system of linear equations over GF(2) in the round key bits. Specifically, for 8 chosen plaintexts, one gets 8 equations; solving them gives the subkeys."

The chosen plaintexts for FEAL-8's linear attack were of the form (0, x_i) where x_i are 32-bit masks. The attack computed ciphertext differences and set up equations for the *key XORs*.

From (E1): R1 known in terms of K0.

Actually, I now remember something important about FEAL's linear attack! The attack works by treating each PAIR as generating a linear equation via the *differential of F along the mask*. Specifically, for a known plaintext/ciphertext pair (P,C), and the key K, consider the "linearized" value:

For the F function, define the "dual" linear map. Actually, here's the key insight I've been circling:

**F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ mask) = const** means we can compute F(x) if we know F(x⊕mask). In the cipher, the round function uses F(R⊕K). If we choose a plaintext such that in some round, the input to F is x, and in another (related) plaintext, the input is x⊕mask, then the *difference* of the round outputs is known. This can be used to **encrypt two chosen plaintexts and compute the value of an intermediate state WITHOUT keys**.

Specifically, consider two plaintexts P1, P2 such that:
- P1 and P2 differ only in such a way that at round 1, the F input difference is mask A (0x00008080). Then the round-1 output difference is D_A (0x00000002). 
- Then at round 2, the F input difference is ... (not exact), so we lose exactness.

But maybe we only need exactness for a *couple* of rounds to compute *one* intermediate state, then peel. Since the exactness breaks, the historical attack uses *probabilistic* continuation or iterates over a small leftover.

State transition:

S4=(L4,R4)=(R3, R2^F(R3^K3))

R3=CR^CL, R4=CL
L3=R2

L1=A=R2^F(R1^K1)   [from R2=A^F(R1^K1)]
L0=R1^F(A^K0) ... 

Keys in terms of states:

K0 = F^{-1}(L0^R1)^A
K1 = F^{-1}(A^R2)^R1
K2 = F^{-1}(R1^R3)^R2
K3 = F^{-1}(R2^R4)^R3

Now, here's a beautiful observation: **For a single pair, (R1,R2) fully determines all four keys.** And the keys must be valid 20-bit-seed expansions. If we consider TWO pairs, the same keys must be produced. The "linear attack" essentially finds (R1,R2) consistent across all pairs.

F^{-1}(L0_j^R1_j)^A_j = F^{-1}(L0_k^R1_k)^A_k = K0 for all j,k.

This means R1_j is constrained by K0: R1_j = L0_j ^ F(A_j^K0) (as we know).

Similarly R2_j is constrained by K1 and K2, etc.

**Let me actually recall the FEAL linear attack now for real.** I'm going to reconstruct from the mathematical principle:

The exact differentials give, for F:
F(x) ⊕ F(x⊕A) = D_A, F(x)⊕F(x⊕B)=D_B.

Now consider the cipher equations between two *related* plaintext/ciphertext pairs. For ANY two pairs (not necessarily chosen), let's XOR the equations:

For pair j and pair k:
R2_j ⊕ R2_k = [R4_j⊕R4_k] ⊕ [F(R3_j⊕K3) ⊕ F(R3_k⊕K3)].

This is an exact equation (no approximation) — it's just algebra. The nonlinearity is in F(R3_j⊕K3)⊕F(R3_k⊕K3).

The "linear attack" uses the *differential* to *approximate* this: if R3_j⊕R3_k happened to be A or B, the F-difference is constant. For random pairs, R3_j⊕R3_k is random, so this doesn't help.

So S2 from ciphertext via (K2,K3); from plaintext via (K0,K1).

**MITM on S2 with 2^20 + 2^20 using a hash on intermediate:**

Actually, what if we iterate (K0) → S1 (2^20), and (K2) → "partial" from ciphertext? For each K2, from ciphertext we can't get S2 without K3, but we can get part of it: R2 depends on K3; L2 depends on K2 and R2.

Alternatively, iterate (K宿) → ...

R2 from plaintext = A ⊕ F(R1⊕K1) (K0,K1).
R2 from ciphertext = R4 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3) (K3).

Match R2: A ⊕ F(R1⊕K1) = R4 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3). (Relates K0,K1,K3.) K2 only appears via L2 matching which we haven't used.

L2 from plaintext = R1 (K0).
L2 from ciphertext = R3 ⊕ F(R2⊕K2) (K2 with R2 from ciphertext).

So full S2 match gives two equations:
(i) R1 = R3 ⊕ F(R2^C ⊕ K2), where R2^C = R4 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3).
(ii) A ⊕ F(R1⊕K1) = R2^C.

Equation (ii) relates K0,K1,K3 (no K2). Equation (i) relates K0,K2,K3.

If we iterate K0 (2^20): R1 known.
- (ii): F(R1⊕K1) = A ⊕ R4 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3). So K1⊕R1 = F^{-1}(A⊕R4⊕F(R3⊕K3)) and K2 appears...

The FEAL linear attack:

# 1. Find K3
#    For i in range(2^16):
#        guess part of K3
#        decrypt the last round for all pairs
#        check that the resulting R6 (after removing last round) satisfies a linear relation

Wait, the check: after removing the last round, the *resulting plaintext side* would be a *linear combination* because the remaining rounds satisfy a linear relation via the differentials.

Hmm, let me think about the *remaining 3 rounds*: if we knew K3asi and peeled the last round, we'd have (L3,R3) for each pair, i.e., a "3-round cipher" from plaintext to S3 with keys K0,K1,K2. Is the 3-round version weaker? It's a 3-round Feistel.

For a 3-round Feistel with known plaintext/ciphertext (where "ciphertext" is S3), there's a classic **2^40 attack**... but with the differential maybe 2^20.

Honestly, I think the cleanest reliable path is to **implement an optimized C meet-in-the-middle that solves for all keys with ~2^40 iterations but cleverly reduced via the 20-bit seeds**. Let me estimate the actual runtime more carefully and decide.

Actually, wait! Let me reconsider the problem size. The keys are 20-bit seeds: K_i = expand(s_i). expand is deterministic. For a given K2 candidate (2^20), K1 and K3 derivations are *forced* by pair consistency, but the derived K1,K3 must be *valid seeds* (in the image of expand over 20-bit inputs). The image of expand might be small! Let me check: expand(s) = s ^ (s<<16) ^ (s*12345), for s in [0,2^20). How many distinct 32-bit values? Likely 2^20 distinct (since it's designed as a key expansion). Each derived K1 from the equations is some 32-bit value; the chance it equals a valid expand(s) is 2^20/2^32 = 2^-12 ≈ 0.00024. This gives a strong filter! 

But more importantly, if we iterate K0 (2^20), the hash-MITM for K3 (2^20) can use these validity filters to speed up... but asymptotically still 2^40.

Let me think about the total time differently. Perhaps the intended attack is:

**For each candidate K0 (2^20), solve the remaining 3-key subproblem with a 2^20 MITM using the hash trick, then verify.** That's 2^40 total. In C, with hash tables, 2^40 might be ~hours. But maybe with the *validity filter* (the derived keys must be valid seeds), we can prune massively and only search for candidates matching 2 pairs / few pairs.

Actually, here's a much better idea: **Use the fact that each derived key must be a valid expand output to uniquely pin K3 for each K0 via a single pair + validity check.**

For fixed K0 and a single pair j, the equations give K1 and K2 as functions of K3 and pair data. The number of K3 values (2^20) yields 2^20 (K1,K2) pairs; each is a random 32-bit pair. The probability that BOTH K1 and K2 are valid expand outputs is (2^20/2^32)^2 = 2^-24 ≈ 6e-8. With 32 pairs, the true (K0,K3) will produce valid (K1,K2) for all 32 pairs. A false K3 for pair j produces valid (K1,K2) with prob ~2^-24, so with ~2^20 false K3 values per K0, expected valid false combos ≈ 2^-4 ≈ 0.0625 per pair. So testing validity of (K1,K2) on ONE pair already filters ~2^20 candidates down to <1 expected false positive! 

This means: for each K0 (2^20), we can find the correct K3 by:
- For each K3 candidate (2^20), compute K1,K2 for pair 0 from equations, and check if both K1 and K2 are valid expand outputs (20-bit preimages).
- The correct (K3 for this K0) will yield valid K1,K2 (essentially uniquely among 2^20 false ones).
Then verify K1,K2 with other pairs.

This is 2^20 K3 checks per K0 with O(1) work each → 2^40 total. Still 2^40 operations, BUT each operation is cheap (a few F computations plus expand-check). In C, 2^40 cheap ops ~ hours. Hmm. Still slow for an interactive session but maybe acceptable for background.

Can we reduce K0 iteration similarly? Symmetric: for each K3 (2^20), find K0 via validity... still 2^40.

Hmm. What if we iterate only TWO keys jointly but use the *single-pair validity* to filter: iterate K0 and K3 such that the derived K1,K2 for a SINGLE pair are valid seeds. For each (K0,K3) (2^40), check validity of (K1,K2) for pair 0: expected false pass 2^-24, so the number of valid (K0,K3) pairs is (2^40)×2^-24 ≈ 2^16 = 65536 candidate (K0,K3) combos. Then we can verify each candidate with the remaining pairs. 

But enumerating 2^40 (K0,K3) pairs to find 2^16 candidates requires 2^40 work. Unless we use a **birthday/table**:

Precompute for pair 0: for each K3 (2^20), compute (K1,K2) as a function of K0 → but it also depends on K0 (through R1_0). So (K0,K3) both matter. To find (K0,K3) where (K1,K2) are both valid seeds, we could:
- For each K0 (2^20), for each K3 (2^20): 2^40 computation. No good.

But maybe we can **separate K1 and K2 validity**:
Given K0, pair 0:
K1 = F^{-1}(A_0 ⊕ R2_0) ⊕ R1_0, where R2_0 = R4_0 ⊕ F(R3_0⊕K3).
K2 = F^{-1}(R1_0⊕R3_0) ⊕ R2_0.

Notice K2 depends on R2_0 which depends on K3; K1 also depends on R2_0. Both share R2_0. Define X = R2_0 (a 32-bit value). Then:
K1 = F^{-1}(A_0⊕X) ⊕ R1_0
K2 = F^{-1}(R1_0⊕R3_0) ⊕ X
and X = R4_0 ⊕ F(R3_0⊕K3).

For a valid K0, R1_0 is known. The condition "K1 and K2 are valid seeds" is a condition on X (and R1_0). For a random X, the probability both are valid is 2^-24. And X must also be reachable as R4_0⊕F(R3_0⊕K3) for some K3; as K3 ranges over 2^20, X ranges over 2^20 values (each unique since F is invertible: X = R4_0 ⊕ F(R3_0⊕K3) is a bijection in K3rit K3→X is bijective (F bijective + XOR constants)). So for each K0, the set of reachable X has size 2^20. Among them, the number where both K1,K2 are valid is ~2^20 × 2^-24 = 2^-4 ≈ 0.06 — so for a WRONG K0, likely NO X (i.e., no K3) yields valid (K1,K2). For the CORRECT K0, exactly one X (the true R2_0) yields valid (K1,K2). 

So: **for each K0, test whether there EXISTS K3 such that (K1,K2) valid; this is a 2^20 search per K0 → 2^40 total.** The filtering is strong but we still iterate all K0.

The only way to avoid iterating all K0 is to invert the K0→R1_0 mapping... but R1_0 = L0_0 ⊕ F(A_0⊕K0) is a bijection in K0 (F invertible), so K0 ranges over a full 2^20-sized set. The condition for existence of valid (K1,K2) is a predicate on K0 with ~2^20/16 ≈ 65536 true values (for wrong K0 through X=... wait we need to compute the expected number of K0 that accidentally have some valid X).

Hmm, let me compute more carefully: For a wrong K0, the probability that SOME X (among 2^20 reachable values) yields both K1,K2 valid is ≈ 2^20 × 2^-24 = 2^-4 ≈ 0.0625. So among 2^20 wrong K0 candidates, about 2^16 ≈ 65536 pass the single-pair filter. The correct K0 also passes. So after this two-key (K0,K3) filter using ONE pair, we have ~65536 candidate (K0,K3) combos, and we can verify each against the remaining 31 pairs cheaply!

But we still need to *find* these 65536 candidates without 2^40 work. The predicate is 2^20 per K0 → 2^40 total. Unless we use the structure to invert.

For each K0, the "existence" condition is: ∃ X ∈ reachable-set such that K1(X) valid AND K2(X) valid. Both K1,K2 are functions of X (for fixed K0). We want the intersection of:
- K1(X) ∈ ValidKeys (2^20 targets)
- K2(X) ∈ ValidKeys
where X ranges over the image Set = {R4_0 ⊕ F(R3_0⊕K3) : K3}.

Since Set has 2^20 elements and ValidKeys has 2^20, expected intersection K1(X) ∩ ValidKeys has size ~2^20×2^20/2^32 = 2^8 = 256; then K2 on those 256, expect 256×2^20/2^32 = 0.0625 → matches 0.0625 candidates per K0.

To compute this efficiently for each K0, we can precompute tables ONCE (independent of K0):
- For each K3, X = R4_0 ⊕ F(R3_0⊕K3). Store X.
- ValidKeys: the set of expand(s) for s<2^20. As a hash set (2^20 entries).

Then for each K0: compute R1_0, then for each X in precomputed list (2^20), compute K1=..., K2=... and check membership in ValidKeys. That's 2^20 per K0 → 2^40. 

Is there a way to invert "K1 valid" to limit X? K1 = F^{-1}(A_0⊕X)⊕R1_0 ∈ ValidKeys ⇒ X = A_0 ⊕ F(R1_0 ⊕ k) for some valid k. So for fixed K0, the set of X that make K1 valid has size 2^20 (one per valid key). Then among those X, K2 must be valid. Check K2 for those 2^20 X. Still 2^20 per K0.

Hmm, 2^40 remains the wall.

Let me consider the possibility that the intended solution ACTUALLY IS 2^40 in optimized C (e.g., with early abort + single-pair filtering, maybe ~10^12 cheap ops ≈ 1-2 hours in C). For a coding challenge, "implement" suggests the solution should run rather quickly though.

For K0, compute R1_0. Then define the mapping X → (K1,K2):
K1(X) = F^{-1}(A_0⊕X)⊕R1_0
K2(X) = F^{-1}(R1_0⊕R3_0)⊕X

We need K1(X), K2(X) ∈ ValidKeyshare and X ∈ Set (reachable).

So for the true K0, X_TRUE ∈ S1(K0) ∩ Set. For a wrong K0, expected |S1∩Set| = 2^20×2^20/2^32 = 2^8. Small but nonempty.

We need to find K0 such that S1(K0) ∩ Set contains an X with K2(X) valid.
