No embedded key. Let me now really focus on the FEAL linear attack algorithm. Let me try to recall it by thinking about what "linear attack" means in cryptography for FEAL.

**The FEAL Linear Attack (from Biham & Shamir's "Linear Cryptanalysis of FEAL"):**

I'm now recalling the actual structure! It's based on the observation:

For FEAL, F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x80800000) = 0x02000000 and F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x00008080) = 0x00000002.

The cipher is built as a Feistel network. The attack:
1. Choose plaintexts P_i such that P_i XOR P_j gives a specific difference pattern at each round (a "characteristic").
2. Because F differences are known (with probability 1 along the characteristic), the ciphertext differences are known.
3. This allows computing the **XOR of certain intermediate values**, e.g., R2^(i) XOR R2^(j).
4. From these XORs)Skip, you can compute the *difference of F inputs* at a round, leading to equations involving key bytes.
5. After peeling rounds, recover the full keys.

The "linear" in the name: the characteristic treats the cipher as linear at the level of the differential masks.

Since we have random pairs, maybe the challenge intends: **use the exact differential on the *last round* to determine K3 via a 2^20 brute force, where the check uses a linear relation in the R2 values of two pairs.**

Let me try to derive: If we guess K3, we get R2_j for all pairs by R2_j = CL_j ⊕ F(R3_j ⊕ K3). Now, from the PLAINTEXT side, R2_j = A_j ⊕ F(R1_j ⊕ K1) where R1_j = L0_j ⊕ F(A_j⊕K0). 

For the CORRECT K3, the R2_j values are the true intermediate values, which are "reachable" from plaintexts via rounds 1-2 with consistent K0,K1. For a WRONG K3, the R2_j values are inconsistent random values; specifically, R2_j should satisfy R2_j = A_j ⊕ F(R1_j⊕K1) for a common K1 across pairs.

So for a candidate K3, for each pair compute R2_j. Then we need K1 such that F(R1_j⊕K1) = A_j ⊕ R2_j for all j. This requires R1_j = L0_j ⊕ F(A_j⊕K0) with a common K0 too. So checking K3 still involves K0,K1.

BUT: here's a **single-pair check**: For each pair, given candidate K3 and R2_j, the value S_j = A_j ⊕ R2_j must equal F(R1_j⊕K1) for a single shared K1. But R1_j also depends on K0. So we have unknowns K0,K1. For a single pair, unknown K0,K1 with two constraints... not enough.

I keep going in circles. Let me just try implementing the **2^40 approach with strong filtering in C** and measure speed. If it's too slow, I'll think more.

Actually, wait. Let me reconsider the time. We found the fundamental search is over (K0, K3) — 2^40. But maybe we can reduce to (K0, K1) = (2^20, 2^20) with a *different* parametrization, and the linear attack's contribution is to note that **R1_j and R2_j (intermediate states) for a SINGLE pair determine all four keys, and cross-pair consistency can be checked with just 2 pairs** — reducing the number of pairs needed, not the search space.

Alternatively, maybe the "linear attack" phrase in the problem statement is a *hint at the exact differential* but the actual expected solution uses the differential to **cut the keyspace by 4 factors**: each round key's *top bits* of specific bytes are recoverable linearly, reducing each key to ~12 bits; then 4×12... no.

Let me analyze the exact differential's implication for key bits more concretely. The differential F(x)⊕F(x⊕B)=D_B (where B=0x80800000 flips bit7 of bytes 2,3) involves the *most significant bits* of the input. This suggests that **bit7 of byte3 of the F output is (bit6 of byte3 of input) XOR (bit7 of bytes 2,3) XOR ...** with carries from lower bits. Not clean.

Let me search for *higher-order* exact linear relations: relations of the form XOR of several output bits = XOR of several input bits, holding with probability 1. Let me search empirically: for each output bit j, find if the XOR of ALL input bits, or input bits in certain positions, predicts it with probability 1. My earlier single-bit test found nothing; let me test 2-input-bit XOR relations: out_j = in_i ⊕ in_k ⊕ const with prob 1. And more generally linear masks: for each output bit j, find subsets of input bits (e.g., all pairs, triples) that correlate perfectly. With 32 input bits, the number of subsets is huge, but we can use the Walsh-Hadamard? Too much.

Alternatively, use the known structure: the exact differentials imply the *second bit* (bit 1) of output byte 3 equals bit7 of input byte 2 ⊕ bit7 of input byte 3 ⊕ bit1-of-something? Let me derive analytically.

y3 = rol2[x3 + y2] where y2 = rol2[(x3^x2)+(x1^x0)+1].
Output bit 1 of y3 (which in the 32-bit output is bit 25) = rol2's input bit-... rol2 maps input bit 7? Let's recall rol2(y) = (y<<2)|(y>>6): output bits:
out bit 0 = in bit 6
out bit 1 = in bit 7
out bit 2 = in bit 0
out bit 3 = in bit 1
...
out bit 7 = in bit 5.
So output bit 1 (of y3) = input bit 7 of (x3 + y2 mod 256), i.e., the top bit (MSB) of the byte sum x3 + y2.

The MSB of an 8-bit sum a+b (mod 256) = bit7 of (a+b). This is 1 iff a+b ≥ 128. That's 1 if MSB(a) XOR MSB(b) XOR carry-into-bit7... Specifically, bit7 of (a+b mod 256) = MSB(a) XOR MSB(b) XOR C7 where C7 is the carry into position 7 (i.e., carry from lower 7 bits). This is a complex function of lower bits. So output bit 25 (bit1 of y3) = MSB(x3) ⊕ MSB(y2) ⊕ carry7.

This is where the differential comes from: flipping MSB of x3 or y2 toggles output bit 1 unless carries change. The exactness of the differential relied on cancellation in y2.

The last round: R2 = R4 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3). If we knew R2 for two pairs, we could solve for K3. The differential helps compute R2 differences... 

Actually, maybe the attack computes R2 (not R2 differences) from the PLAINTEXT side using the *differential* rather than the actual rounds. Let me reconsider the exact differential as a *decryption* tool:

The exact differential F(x)⊕F(x⊕B)=D_B means that in the round function, if the input to F differs by B, the output differs by D_B. Conversely, if the *outputs* of two F evaluations differ by D_B, the *inputs* differ by B (up to... well, if F(y⊕B)=F(y)⊕D_B and F is bijective, then F^{-1}(F(y)⊕D_B) = y⊕B). 

So: if we had two F outputs differing by D_B, we'd know their inputs differ by B. This could help identify keys.

For the cipher: R2_j ⊕ R2_k = L3_j ⊕ L3_k (left halves before round 4). If we knew that (R2_j ⊕ R2_k) = D_B, then F inputs R3_j⊕K3 and R3_k⊕K3 would differ by B, giving R3_j⊕R3_k = B — which we can check from ciphertexts! 

So the *given* pairs might have ciphertexts where some R3 differences equal B or A! Wait, we already checked: no two pairs have R3 differences equal to A or B. But maybe the ciphertext FILE (100 ciphertexts to be decrypted) has such relations? Doesn't help recover key from pairs.

Let me search for the actual FEAL linear attack in the form of a documented algorithm. I'll try to recall the "Linear Attack on FEAL-8" steps from a paper:

```
1. Choose 8 plaintexts P_j such that their left halves are 0 and right halves are masks.
2. Encrypt all, get C_j.
3. Compute the "linearized" values.
4. Recover the extended keys.
```

The attack is a chosen-plaintext attack. Our problem says "known-plaintext attack" — but maybe the pairs.txt SP was generated with chosen plaintexts (the masks), and since we're told they're "plaintext-ciphertext pairs", we can just USE them as chosen pairs! From the earlier output, plaintexts are random-looking 64-bit values, not (0, x). So they're random.

BUT the pairs might have been generated with random plaintexts, and the challenge wants us to use the differentials in a *statistical* way across 32 random pairs. Let me estimate: with 32 random pairs, the differential-based linear attack gives equations that hold with probability ~1/2 (e.g., the linear approximation). To recover 4×20=80 bits of key, we'd need ~80 equations. Each pair might give a few biased equations → 32 pairs might barely suffice if biases are strong.

The "linear approximation" of F (addition≈XOR) holds per byte with some probability. Let me measure: for random inputs, the probability that F(x) = L(x)⊕c exactly was ~0 (we saw 0/20000). But *individual bits* might have high correlation. For linear cryptanalysis, we need a relation like: for a fixed key, some XOR combination of ciphertext bits equals some XOR combination of plaintext bits ⊕ key bits with probability 1/2 + ε. With 32 samples, ε must be > ~0.09 for 95% confidence.

Let me look at this from the perspective of the F function being a **finite permutation** with 32-bit input. The exact differentials give F an "affine" structure. Perhaps we can use them to compute the *intermediate half* values in a *known plaintext attack* via the following:

For each pair, we have A = R0^L0 (plaintext) and R3,R4 (ciphertext). From round 4: R2 = R4 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3). Unknown K3. But maybe there's enough structure in the *given* 32 pairs to determine K3 via a *differential between pairs* (even though R3 differences aren't exactly A/B). 

Let me consider the attack where we **enumerate K3 (2^20) and check consistency using a linear relation on the R2 values across pairs that holds regardless of K0,K1,K2**. Is there such a relation?

From the plaintext side, R2_j = A_j ⊕ F(R1_j⊕K1), R1_j = L0_j ⊕ F(A_j⊕K0). For the TRUE K3, R2_j values are consistent with *some* (K0,K1). For a false K3, the R2_j values are essentially random 32-bit values (because F(R3_j⊕K3) with wrong K3 is pseudorandom). So no simple relation.

OK, the fundamental issue is that two keys are entangled. The linear attack MUST break this entanglement. Let me think about whether the exact differentials break the entanglement.

Given the exact differential F(x)⊕F(x⊕B)=D_B, can we replace F in the equations with a *linear function* on the relevant *bit* so that the two-key entanglement becomes a single-key equation?

Let me look at the equation F(R1_j ⊕ K1) = N_j ⊕ K2. Both sides involve K2. The differential might let us "move" K2 into a different position. Hmm.

Actually WAIT. I just remembered the actual FEAL linear attack! It uses **the parity (XOR) of F over the subgroups generated by the masks**. Here's the idea:

Because F(x)⊕F(x⊕A)=D_A and F(x)⊕F(x⊕B)=D_B, the function F induces a well-defined *linear map* from the quotient group (Z2^32 / G) hmm... 

Actually here's the cleaner statement: The four masks {0,A,B,A⊕B} form an affine subspace on which F is "linear": F(x)⊕F(x⊕m) = L(m) for m in the subgroup. This means for any x, F restricted to the coset {x, x⊕A, x⊕B, x⊕A⊕B} is a *linear function of the coset element* (up to flips). 

This allows the following: define for any 32-bit value x, its "signature" under the subgroup: the 2-bit value (x·u, x·v) where u = A-ish... The linear attack likely considers each pair and derives equations on these signatures.

OK, fundamental barrier. Let me try to think about **the actual FEAL linear attack's core trick** once more, because I know it exists and breaks this type of entanglement.

The core trick (from the FEAL linear attack paper by Biham & Shamir, 1991): 

"Since F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x80800000) = 0x02000000, we can find the last subkey by checking all 2^16 possibilities for the right half of the last subkey, using a **linear relation** that holds for the *first 3 rounds*."

Wait. The linear relation for the first 3 rounds: For ANY plaintext/ciphertext pair (with the last round peeled), the *input to round 2* (i.e., R1) can be computed from the plaintext using K0. And from the peeled ciphertext, R1 can be computed from the other side using ... no.

Hmm, actually here's the cleanest known FEAL linear attack (for FEAL-8, 8 rounds):

1. Peel the last round by guessing K7 (32-bit, or in chunks). For each guess, compute the 64-bit value entering round 8 for each pair.
2. These values should satisfy a *linear relationship* with the plaintexts because the first 7 rounds (with K0..K6) form a *linear* transformation *when restricted to the chosen plaintext pattern*... 

Actually no — the LINEAR ATTACK is not about peeling; it's about the fact that the F function can be *linearized* on a *2-element orbit*, allowing the attacker to compute intermediate differences.

F function runs at ~156 Mops/s in C. So 2^40 F operations ≈ 1.1e12 / 1.56e8 ≈ 7,000 seconds ≈ 2 hours. That's too slow for repeated interaction, but maybe acceptable if we run in the background? Not great.

But wait, we don't need to do full F evaluation per candidate; with SIMD or optimized loops we could push higherents. Also, the search might be reducible.

Let me reconsider. Maybe the "linear attack" actually reduces the search to ~2^21 total using a **hash table over intermediate states** that I haven't fully exploited. Let me think about a **double MITM** structure:

We want to find keys such that for pair 0 the derived keys are valid. Let me use the (U,X) parametrization:

U = R1_0 (depends on K0), X = R2_0 (depends on K3).

The keys in terms of (U,X) for pair 0:
K0 = F^{-1}(L0_0 ⊕ U) ⊕ A_0
K1 = F^{-1}(A_0 ⊕ X) ⊕ U
K2 = F^{-1}(U ⊕ R3_0) ⊕ X
K3 = F^{-1}(X ⊕ R4_0) ⊕ R3_0

(A_0, L0_0, R3_0, R4_0 are pair-0 constants.)

Constraints: all four keys ∈ ValidKeys (V).

We need to find (U,X) satisfying the four "∈V" constraints, then verify with other pairs.

Now here's the key idea for a MITM: separate the constraints into those involving U and X:
- K0 ∈ V: U = L0_0 ⊕ F(A_0 ⊕ v0) for some v0 ∈ V. (U only)
- K1 ∈ V: F^{-1}(A_0 ⊕ X) ⊕ U ∈ V ⇔ U ∈ V ⊕ F^{-1}(A_0⊕X) ⇔ U = v1 ⊕ F^{-1}(A_0⊕X) for some v1∈V. (both U and X)
- K2 ∈ V: F^{-1}(U ⊕ R3_0) ⊕ X ∈ V ⇔ X = F^{-1}(U ⊕ R3_0) ⊕ v2. (both)
- K3 ∈ V: X = R4_0 ⊕ F(R3_0 ⊕ v3) for some v3∈V. (X only)

So the four constraints:
(C0) U ∈ {L0_0 ⊕ F(A_0⊕v) : v∈V} =: S0 (size 2^20)
(C3) X ∈ {R4_0 ⊕ F(R3_0⊕v) : v∈V} =: S3 (size 2^20)
(C1) U ∈ ⋃_{x∈S3-like} (V ⊕ F^{-1}(A_0⊕X)) [couples U,X]
(C2) X ∈ V ⊕ F^{-1}(U⊕R3_0) [couples]

We can do a MITM:
- Enumerate v1 ∈ V (2^20): compute F^{-1}(A_0⊕X) = U⊕v1 → X = A_0 ⊕ F(U⊕v1). So for each (v1, U), X is determined. Hmm.

Alternative MITM: we want pairs (U,X) satisfying C1 and C2, with U∈S0 and X∈S3.

Let me define for each U ∈ S0 (2^20 values), the set of X satisfying C1: {X = A_0 ⊕ F(U⊕v1) : v1 ∈ V} =: T1(U) (size 2^20). The set of X satisfying C2: {X = F^{-1}(U⊕R3_0) ⊕ v2 : v2 ∈ V} =: T2(U) (size 2^20). We need X ∈ S3 ∩ T1(U) ∩ T2(U).

For the TRUE (U*,X*): X* ∈ S3 ∩ T1(U*) ∩ T2(U*).
For a wrong U, expected |S3 ∩ T1 ∩ T2| = 2^20 × (2^20/2^32)^2 = 2^20 × 2^-24 = 2^-4. So almost no wrong U passes. So we just need to find the U ∈ S0 for which S3∩T1∩T2 nonempty.

Computing T1∩T2∩S3 for each U naively is 2^20 per U → 2^40. BUT we can use hashing:

T1(U) = A_0 ⊕ F(U⊕v1) for v1∈V. This set depends on U.
T2(U) = F^{-1}(U⊕R3_0) ⊕ V (a coset of V shifted by w(U)=F^{-1}(U⊕R3_0)).

Hmm, T2(U) is the set {w(U)⊕v : v∈V} — a "random" 32-bit set of size 2^20, which can be thought of as a hash table for membership testing (2^20 entries).

So for each U, we want to find X ∈ S3 such that X ∈ T2(U) AND X ∈ T1(U). 

We can test each X ∈ S3 (2^20 values) for membership in T2(U) using a hash table (O(1)), AND check T1(U) membership by computing A_0⊕F(U⊕v1)... hmm T1(U) is defined as {A_0⊕F(U⊕v1)}; membership of a given X in T1(U) requires X⊕A_0 ∈ F(U⊕V) ⇔ F^{-1}(X⊕A_0) ∈ U⊕V ⇔ U ∈ F^{-1}(X⊕A_0) ⊕ V. So testing X ∈ T1(U) can be done via a hash lookup on V as well!

So: For each U ∈ S0 (2^20):
  Build hash set T2(U) = {w(U)⊕v : v∈V} (2^20 entries) — expensive to rebuild per U (2^20 × 2^20).
  OR iterate X ∈ S3 (2^20) and test both memberships:
    - X ∈ T2(U): X ⊕ w(U) ∈ V → hash lookup in V.
    - X ∈ T1(U): U ∈ F^{-1}(X⊕A_0) ⊕ V → U ⊕ F^{-1}(X⊕A_0) ∈ V → hash lookup in V.
  So per (U, X): two hash lookups. Total 2^20 × 2^20 = 2^40 hash lookups. STILL 2^40.

The cardinality of the search space is inherently 2^40 (U,X), because both U and X have 2^20 possibilities each. There's no way around it unless we find an algebraic relation that reduces the combined degrees of freedom. The constraints are 4 set-memberships linking U and X through F, which is nonlinear. So I believe 2^40 is the natural search space.

BUT: 2^40 with *hash lookups* (~5ns each) ≈ 5500 s ≈ 1.5 hours. Too slow for this environment (could time out).

Hold on, maybe we can reduce U's search space below 2^20. U = R1_0 = L0_0 ⊕ F(A_0⊕K0), as K0 ranges over 2^20 seeds, U takes 2^20 distinct values (bijective). So U space is inherently 2^20. X = R2_0 = R4_0 ⊕ F(R3_0⊕K3) also has 2^20 distinct values. So the (U,X) space is 2^40. To do better, we need to avoid iterating over the full (U,X) grid.

Use the structural hash: We want the existence of (U,X) such that:
- U ∈ S0, X ∈ S3
- X ∈ T1(U) ⇔ U ∈ P(X) := V ⊕ F^{-1}(X⊕A_0)
- X ∈ T2(U) ⇔ X ⊕ w(U) ∈ V where w(U)=F^{-1}(U⊕R3_0)

So conditions:
1. U ∈ S0 ∩ P(X)
2. X ∈ S3 ∩ (V ⊕ w(U))

We can MITM: build a hash table over U ∈ S0:
  For each U ∈ S0, compute w(U) = F^{-1}(U⊕R3_0) and the coset C(U) = V ⊕ w(U) (a set). We want some X ∈ S3 ∩ C(U) such that U ∈ P(X).

Alternative: build a hash table over X ∈ S3:
  For each X ∈ S3, compute P(X) = V ⊕ F^{-1}(X⊕A_0) (a set). We want some U ∈ S0 ∩ P(X) such that X ∈ C(U).

This is a mutual membership problem. We can solve it with a hash table storing one side's data:

Approach: 
1. Build a hash table from X-values to the corresponding data? But we need to query "is there an X ∈ S3 such that U∈P(X) and X∈C(U)?".

For each U ∈ S0 (2^20), we need to find X ∈ S3 ∩ C(U) ∩ {X : U∈P(X)}.
Equivalently X ∈ S3 ∩ C(U) with U ∈ P(X) ⇔ X ∈ S3 ∩ C(U) AND X ∈ {X' : U∈P(X')}.
The set {X' : U∈P(X')} = {X : U ∈ V⊕F^{-1}(X⊕A_0)} = {X : F^{-1}(X⊕A_0) ∈ U⊕V} = {X : X⊕A_0 ∈ F(U⊕V)} = {X : X ∈ A_0 ⊕ F(U⊕v), v∈V} = T1(U).

So we're back to X ∈ S3 ∩ C(U) ∩ T1(U).

To make this fast, build a hash table for T1(U) for each U? That's the expensive part.

But observe T1(U) = A_0 ⊕ F(U⊕V). Both C(U) = V ⊕ w(U) and T1(U) are sets derived from V. The intersection S3 ∩ C(U) ∩ T1(U) — with S3 also from V. All sets are "V-related". The probability of nonempty is ~2^-4 as computed, so for most U it's empty. We need a way to *probe* emptiness faster than scanning all X ∈ S3.

Idea: For a given U, S3 ∩ C(U) has expected size 2^20×2^20/2^32 = 2^8. We could find S3 ∩ C(U) by iterating over X ∈ S3 and checking X⊕w(U) ∈ V: 2^20 lookups per U. That's the 2^40.

Alternatively iterate over X∈S3 for the OUTER loop: for each X∈S3 (2^20), we need U∈S0 ∩ P(X) such that X∈C(U). S0∩P(X) expected size 2^8. Then we need to check X∈C(U) ⇔ X⊕w(U)∈V. So for each X, iterate ~2^8 candidate U's (from S0∩P(X)) and do O(1) check. But finding S0∩P(X) requires iterating S0 (2^20 per X) → 2^40.

Hmm. Unless we *precompute* P(X) sets... they depend on X.

We can build a hash table mapping Y = F^{-1}(X⊕A_0) (for X∈S3) to the list of X values: 2^20 entries. Then for each U ∈ S0 (2^20):
  Compute t = F^{-1}(U⊕? no). Wait P(X) = {U : U ∈ V ⊕ Y(X)} ⇔ U⊕Y(X) ∈ V.
  For each U, we want X∈S3 with U⊕Y(X) ∈ V and X⊕w(U) ∈ V.
  Build hash over X∈S3 keyed by Y(X): Given U, for each v∈V we need Y = U⊕v and X such that Y(X)=Y. So:
    - For each v∈V (2^20), Y = U⊕v; look up Y in hash → list of X; for those X check X⊕w(U)∈V.
  Per U: 2^20 v-lookups → 2^40 total. Ugh.

OK here's the thing: no matter how we slice it, the joint (U,X) space has 2^40 cells and the constraints are sparse. The 2^40 seems irreducible without exploiting F's algebraic structure (the linear attack).

Let me really find the linear attack. I'll try to recall the exact equations from the FEAL linear attack paper by searching my memory of the *CryptoHack FEAL solution*. I remember there's a known solution for "FEAL - Linear attack" challenge on CryptoHack:

The linear attack on FEAL-4 (from CryptoHack writeups):
```
The F function has the property F(x) ^ F(x ^ 0x80800000) = 0x02000000.
This means that if we take plaintexts P1 = L|R and P2 = L|(R ^ 0x80800000), 
their ciphertexts C1,C2 differ in specific predictable ways.
...
We can use this to recover subkeys one chunk at a time.
```

Since this challenge says "known-plaintext" though with 32 random pairs, maybe the intended use is: **each pair can be used to derive a linear equation between key chunks using the differential property applied to the pair's own ciphertext...** 

Hmm, actually wait. Let me reconsider the exact differential in a different light: it's a **differential with probability 1**, so we can use it to *peel off* a round by testing, for each guess of a round key, whether the decrypted result has the expected *difference pattern* with another pair. But as noted, random pairs won't have the expected differences.

Unless we don't need pairs to be related! Here's the trick I think I've been missing:

**The linear attack uses ONE pair at a time and the KEY's structure (20-bit seed), not pairs relationships.**

For a single pair, given a guessed K3, we compute R2 = R4 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3). For the TRUE K3, R2 is the true state. But how to check TRUE without other keys?

Maybe we use the fact that R2 must equal A ⊕ F(R1⊕K1) AND A = R2 ⊕ F(R1⊕K1) where R1 = L0 ⊕ F(A⊕K0). This involves K0,K1. So single-pair check needs K0,K1.

BUT for a single pair, we can *enumerate K0* (2^20) and then for each K0, compute R1 and check if there EXISTS K1,K2,K3 that explain the ciphertext. I think I should look at this from a totally different angle: maybe the challenge's "linear attack" refers to a straightforward **Gaussian elimination over GF(2) on a linearized version of the cipher where each pair gives linear equations, and we solve for the 4×20 = 80 key bits**. Since the cipher is small, maybe we can linearize F by treating each byte's addition carries as unknown variables, then solve. This is the "linearization attack": replace each nonlinear operation (modular addition) with its linear parts plus new variables for carries, then solve the resulting linear system over GF(2). This is a known technique!

For a fixed pair, the ciphertext is a nonlinear function of plaintext and key. If we linearize each byte-addition (a+b) as a⊕b⊕c (introducing carry variables c where each carry bit is a new binary variable), then the *entire cipher* for a single pair becomes a system of *linear equations* over GF(2) in the key bits and carry bits! With 32 pairs, we have many equations and variables. Solve with Gaussian elimination to find the key bits (and carry bits as side variables).

This is a classic "algebraic attack" / linearization, and arguably it IS a "linear attack"! The nonlinearity of addition can be linearized by introducing carry variables. Let me develop this:

The cipher operations per byte:
- XOR: linear (over GF(2)).
- rol2: linear (permutation matrix).
- Modular addition mod 256: nonlinear. But we can express a+b mod 256 = a ⊕ b ⊕ c, where c is the carry vector. In fact, for 8-bit addition, the bits satisfy a linear relation with carry variables. More precisely, the bit of (a+b mod 256) is a_i ⊕ b_i ⊕ carry_in addition... The addition of two 8-bit numbers can be fully linearized with carry bits c_i (7 carries) as variables:
  s_i = a_i ⊕ b_i ⊕ c_i, where c_i = (a_{i-1}b_{i-1} ⊕ (a_{i-1}∨b_{i-1})c_{i-1})... The carry recurrence is quadratic! c_i = majority function, which is nonlinear (AND terms). So carries are not free linear variables; they're determined by products of input bits. Linearization of the carry: c_i = a_{i-1} & b_{i-1} ⊕ (a_{i-1} ⊕ b_{i-1}) & c_{i-1}, which contains ANDs (nonlinear). So true linearization would need to introduce variables for every AND product — the system grows but remains solvable via exploiting low degree? This is algebraic cryptanalysis with Gröbner bases... too complex to hand-code, and z3 isn't available.

Alternatively, represent the cipher in GF(2) but treat the addition via its *truth table* as a set of constraints; use SAT. Without z3, writing a SAT solver is no fun.

The statistical linear cryptanalysis approach doesn't look promising. Let me step back and think about what "linear attack" means in the context of THIS specific challenge (with 20-bit seeds and 32 random pairs).

Wait, maybe the "linear attack" refers to a **meet-in-the-middle made linear** where the intermediate state is a linear function of the key due to the F invertibility... Hmm.

Let me reconsider the structure. The key insight from the equations:

Given K0 and K3 (2^40), we can compute K1,K2 for each pair and check consistency. The correct (K0,K3) is found by consistency, and there's exactly one.

What if we use **hash-based MITM to find (K0,K3) in ~2^21 total** by building a table indexed by *derived* (K1,K2) from pair 0, over ALL K0,K3 (which is 2^40 — too many to store).

But we don't need to store all 2^40: we can partition. Actually 2^40 keys (K1,K2) would need storing 2^40 entries to find collisions. Too much memory.

However, we can use the **valid-seed condition** to prune: we only care about (K0,K3) where derived (K1,K2) are BOTH valid seeds. That reduces candidates to ~2^16 (as computed). If we could *generate* those 2^16 candidates without scanning all 2^40...

We showed per (K0,K2) we can derive K3, and valid K3 filters. Let me explore a **hash-join between K0 and K3 via the K1 valid condition**:

For pair 0, condition K1 ∈ V: 
K1 = W(K3) ⊕ U(K0), where W(K3) = F^{-1}(A_0 ⊕ R4_0 ⊕ F(R3_0 ⊕ K3)) and U(K0) = R1_0 = L0_0 ⊕ F(A_0⊕K0).

K1 ∈ V ⇔ W(K3) ⊕ U(K0) ∈ V ⇔ W(K3) ∈ U(K0) ⊕ V.

So for each K3, W(K3) is some 32-bit value. For each K0, U(K0) is some 32-bit value. The condition K1∈V connects them: W(K3) must be in the coset U(K0)⊕V.

Now ALSO condition K2 ∈ V:
K2 = M(K0) ⊕ T(K3), where T(K3) = R4_0 ⊕ F(R3_0⊕K3) and M(K0) = F^{-1}(U(K0)⊕R3_0). 
K2 ∈ V ⇔ M(K0) ⊕ T(K3) ∈ V ⇔ T(K3) ∈ M(K0) ⊕ V.

And the relationship between T and W: T(K3) = R4_0 ⊕ F(R3_0⊕K3); W(K3) = F^{-1}(A_0 ⊕ T(K3)). So given T, W = F^{-1}(A_0⊕T).

So we have a system in (K0, K3) through U,M (functions of K0) and T,W (functions of K3):
(U(K0) ⊕ V) ∋ W(K3)
(M(K0) ⊕ V) ∋ T(K3)

Both are of form "the K3-side value lies in a coset of V determined by K0-side value".

We can build a hash table over K3: key = W(K3) → list of K3 (2^20 entries). Then for each K0 (2^20): for each v∈V (2^20), look up W'(v) = U(K0)⊕v → get candidate K3 list; then check T(K3) ∈ M(K0)⊕V.

Per K0: 2^20 lookups → 2^40 total. Still.

Alternatively build hash over K3 keyed by T(K3), and over K0 consider ... same.

The essential difficulty: the intersection condition on (K0,K3) couples 2^20×2^20 cells, and each cell check is O(1), so 2^40 is the natural complexity. The only way to beat it is if the coupling is "separable" (i.e., the condition factors as f(K0)=g(K3)), which would allow a hash join. Here the conditions don't factor neatly because of the V-coset structure. 

BUT notice: both conditions have the form "value_K3 ∈ coset(K0)" where the coset is V shifted by a K0-dependent value. This is exactly a *join* that CAN be done with two hash tables:

Condition 1: W(K3) ⊕ U(K0) ∈ V.
Condition 2: T(K3) ⊕ M(K0) ∈ V.

Consider building a hash table over K0: for each element v∈V and each K0, the pair (U(K0)⊕v, something)... hmm.

Alternative: iterate over v∈V as a free variable!

From condition 1: W(K3) = U(K0) ⊕ v1, i.e., F^{-1}(A_0⊕T(K3)) = U(K0) ⊕ v1.
From condition 2: T(K3) ⊕ M(K0) = v2 ∈ V.

So we have variables K0, K3, v1, v2 ∈ V with:
(1) F^{-1}(A_0 ⊕ T(K3)) ⊕ U(K0) = v1   [v1 ∈ V]
(2) T(K3) ⊕ M(K0) = v2                 [v2 ∈ V]

We can eliminate K3 via T: T(K3) = R4_0 ⊕ F(R3_0⊕K3) is a bijection from K3∈V to T∈{R4_0⊕F(R3_0⊕v)} (a set of size 2^20). Similarly U(K0), M(K0) from K0.

Now the equations become:
(1) F^{-1}(A_0 ⊕ T) ⊕ U = v1
(2) T ⊕ M = v2
where U=U(K0), M=M(K0), T=T(K3).

From (2): T = M ⊕ v2. From (1): F^{-1}(A_0 ⊕ M ⊕ v2) ⊕ U = v1 ⇒ U = F^{-1}(A_0 ⊕ M ⊕ v2) ⊕ v1.

So given (K0, v2), we can compute T = M⊕v2able, then U must equal F^{-1}(A_0⊕T)⊕v1. And U=U(K0) is already fixed by K0. So condition (1) reduces to a *consistency check* on (K0, v1, v2)! And v1 = U ⊕ F^{-1}(A_0⊕T) is forced.

I think this is overcomplicating. Let me just accept that we need to search 2^40 but make it FAST and run it in the background. Actually wait — 2^40 cells with ~1-2 hash lookups each; at ~10^8 lookups/s, that's 10^4 s ≈ 2.8 hours. Too slow probably for this environment (tool might not allow multi-hour runs). Let me find a way to cut to ~2^32.

**Cut using one more pair!** We have 32 pairs. The single-pair validity gives ~2^16 candidate (K0,K3) combos. If we *generate* those candidates more cleverly... 

Let me think about generating candidates via a **hash join on the K1-valid condition**:

We have 2^20 K0 values and 2^20 K3 values. The condition "K1 valid" alone: W(K3) ∈ U(K0)⊕V. The number of (K0,K3) satisfying this: For each K0, |{K3 : W(K3) ∈ U(K0)⊕V}| ≈ 2^20 × (2^20/2^32) = 2^8. So ~2^20 × 2^8 = 2^28 (K0,K3) pairs satisfy K1∈V.

We can COMPUTE these 2^28 pairs efficiently using a hash join:
- Build table H: for each K3 (2^20), compute W(K3), store mapping W → list of K3. (2^20 entries)
- For each K0 (2^20) and each v∈V (2^20), look up key = U(K0)⊕v in H → list of K3 candidates. That's 2^40 lookups again...

But we can swap: iterate over the 2^20 W-values and the 2^20 U-values:
- W(K3) and U(K0) must satisfy W⊕U ∈ V, i.e., W ∈ U⊕V.
- Number of valid pairs: for each (K0,K3), probability 2^20/2^32 = 2^-12, so 2^20×2^20×2^-12 = 2^28 pairs. To enumerate them: sort the U-values and W-values? The condition "W⊕U ∈ V" connects them through membership in V.

We can build a hash set of V. Then:
- For each K0 (U value) (2^20): for each w∈W-set (2^20): check w⊕U ∈ V → 2^40 checks. Too much.
- Better: For each K3, W known. Then U must be in W⊕V. The set W⊕V has 2^20 elements. So for each K3, iterate 2^20 U's (K0's) and check U ∈ W⊕V via hash on V: Precompute hash of V. For each (K3, K0): check (W(K3)⊕U(K0)) ∈ V — still 2^40.

But we can do better with sorted arrays:
- Sort the list of W-values? We want pairs (U,w) with U⊕w ∈ V. For each U, the set of w such that U⊕w∈V has size 2^20 (since V has 2^20 elements). So total pairs = 2^20 (U's) × 2^20 (w's that match) = 2^40? That contradicts my earlier estimate of 2^28.

Wait: for a fixed U, as w ranges over 2^32 possible values, there are 2^20 values of w with w∈U⊕V. But w only takes 2^20 possible values (one per K3). So expected matches per U = 2^20 × (2^20/2^32) = 2^8. Total pairs = 2^20 × 2^8 = 2^28. ✓.

To enumerate: for each U (2^20), we need to find w in the W-list such that w ∈ U⊕V, i.e., w⊕U ∈ V. Build a hash set of W-values keyed by w (2^20 entries). Then for each U and each v∈V (2^20), look up w = U⊕v in the W hash. That's 2^20 × 2^20 = 2^40 lookups again. BUT we can flip: build a hash set of W-values. Then for each (U, v) pair, w = U⊕v; but that's still 2^40.

Hmm, however, we can build a hash table mapping *coset representatives*: group W-values by their coset modulo V. Since V is a subgroup of (Z2)^32 of size 2^20, the cosets have size 2^20, and there are 2^12 cosets. But wait — is V = {expand(s) : s<2^20} a subgroup? expand is `s ^ (s<<16) ^ (s*12345)`. Is expand a linear (GF(2)-linear) map? Let's check: expand(s) = s ^ (s<<16) ^ (s*12345). The term `s*12345` is an integer multiplication, which involves carries... NOT GF(2)-linear. So V is NOT a subgroup of (Z2)^32. It's just a set of size 2^20. Damn.

If V were a subgroup, the coset structure would let us do hash joins in 2^20. But V is just a random set (expanded key values). So `U⊕V` is a "random-like" set, and membership queries require hash lookups. The cost to find matches between U-set and W-set with constraint w⊕U∈V seems to be 2^40 naively... but there might be a smarter way.

Actually, we can use a *hash table over U⊕W values*: build the set of (U⊕W) for all (K0,K3) pairs = 2^40 values — too many.

Alternatively, since we only need to find candidates where both K1 and K2 are valid, the expected number of solutions is ~2^16 — tiny. We need a "biclique"/hash-join:

Find (K0,K3) such that (W(K3)⊕U(K0)) ∈ V AND (T(K3)⊕M(K0)) ∈ V.

Define two hash tables:
- For each K3: W3, T3 (2^20 entries).
- For each K0: U0, M0 (2^20 entries).

We want pairs with W3⊕U0 ∈ V and T3⊕M0 ∈ V.

This is a standard hash join problem. We can do it by iterating over the smaller effective dimension... but both are 2^20.

Hash join algorithm:
For each element v1 ∈ V (2^20):
  For each K3: candidate U0 = v1 ⊕ W3 (a 2^20 set).
  For each K0: candidate U0' = ... no.

Alternatively, build a hash H mapping from a combined value to candidates. Let me define for each K0, the set of "acceptable T values": T ∈ M0⊕V (2^20). For each K3, check T3 ∈ M0⊕V (needs the K0's list). This is a join between K0-list and K3-list on the value-pairs (U,W) and (M,T).

We can do it with a hash table keyed by (U0 ⊕ W3)? That's 2^40 bits. Not feasible.

Honest assessment: the 2^40 joint search seems to be the bottleneck, and exploiting V as a set of size 2^20 doesn't reduce the *search* below 2^40 because V isn't a linear subspace.

Hmm, "this" refers to the 20-bit seed structure. "This should make it easier for you" — meaning the linear attack becomes *feasible* because after the linear attack recovers *some* key info, the 20-bit seeds make the final search easy. **Plan: Iterate K0 (2^20). For each K0, compute for all 32 pairs: U_j = R1_j = L0_j ⊕ F(A_j⊕K0), M_j = F^{-1}(U_j⊕R3_j), N_j = M_j⊕A_j, P_j = M_j⊕R4_j.

Check if K1,K2,K3 are valid seeds (preimage under expand).

Per K0: 2^20 iterations, each with 2 inverse-F + expand checks. If valid, verify. Expected valid candidates per K0: ~2^-4 from single-pair validity (K1,K2,K3 all valid: (2^20/2^32)^3 = 2^-36, times 2^20 K2 = 2^-16 — too few!). 

Wait, that's for K1,K2,K3 all being valid images of expand. But we're ITERATING K2 as a *seed* (valid by construction since K2=expand(s2)); we check K1 and K3 validity: probability 2^-12 each → 2^-24 per (K0,s2). Times 2^20 s2 → 2^-4 candidates per K0. So per K0, expected 0.0625 candidates (i.e., most K0 yield nothing; the true K0 yields exactly 1). Hence verifying only when a candidate appears (rare) is cheap.

So the total work is 2^20 K0 × 2^20 K2 × ~2 ops = 2^41 ≈ 2.2e12 ops. At ~200M F-ops/s (inverse F is 600M/s), that's ~2.2e12/2e8 = 11,000 s ≈ 3 hours. Too slow for interactive but maybe as a background job... risky.

BUT we can use TWO pairs to reduce K2 search per K0! For pair 0 and pair 1, the K2 that makes (K1,K3) valid for pair 0 must ALSO make them valid for pair 1. We can use a hash-join between pair 0 and pair 1 over K2:

For a given K0:
- For pair 0, for each s2: compute (K1_0, K3_0). Store in hash table keyed by (K1_0, K3_0) → s2. (2^20 entries)
- For pair 1, for each s2: compute (K1_1, K3_1). Look up (K1_1,K3_1) in table. If found, we have a (s2) with consistent (K1,K3) across pairs 0,1.

Then verify across all pairs. The correct K0 yields exactly the true K2 (and K1,K3). A wrong K0 yields (K1,K3) pairs that are essentially random 64-bit values; the chance pair1's (K1,K3) matches any pair0's is 2^20 × 2^-64 ≈ 2^-44 — basically never. So for a wrong K0, no entry in the hash, and the hash check per s2 is O(1).

Per K0: build hash table for pair 0: 2^20 entries (compute K1_0,K3_0 for each s2), then for pair 1: 2^20 lookups. So ~2^21 operations per K0, but with early exit: if pair1 lookup fails for most s2, we do full 2^20 lookups anyway. Total: 2^20 K0 × 2^21 = 2^41 ops. Hmm, same order. But the operations are cheap (2 inverse-F + hash insert/lookup). With inverse-F at 600 Mops/s, and 2 inverse-F per s2... 2^41 × 2 = 4.4e12 F-inv = 7300 s. Still ~2 hours.

Can we cut K0 by using a hash-join over K0 too? For a given s2 (K2 value), the derived (K1,K3) depends on K0 through U_j and N_j/P_j. So for pair 0, (K0, s2) → (K1,K3). Define the hash table over (K0,s2) — that's 2^40 pairs → too much.

So K1 and K2 are *directly computable* from (S1,S3) — no MITM needed! (Because the middle is only 2 rounds, given both endpoints we can solve directly.)

We need to find the unique (K0,K3) such that the derived K1,K2 (and the implied K0,K3 themselves) are all valid seeds and consistent across 32 pairs. The equality condition has probability ~2^-64 (K1,K2 each 32-bit). So pair1 eliminates all false candidates. So 2^16 candidates × 31 pairs verification = cheap.)

So the workload is dominated by the 2^40 (K0,K3) enumeration. To avoid enumerating all (K0,K3) pairs, we must use the validity structure to *generate* candidates. As discussed, the candidates are (K0,K3) such that for pair 0, K1,K2 are valid. 

Let me focus on generating these 2^16 candidates without 2^40 work.

Given pair 0 data, we want (K0,K3) such that:
K1 = F^{-1}(A_0⊕X)⊕U ∈ V  where U=R1_0 (from K0), X=R2_0=R4_0⊕F(R3_0⊕K3).
K2 = F^{-1}(U⊕R3_0)⊕X ∈ V.

And U ranges over 2^20 values (from K0), X over 2^20 values (from K3).

We need to efficiently find all (U,X) in the 2^40 grid satisfying the two V-memberships.

**Hash join on (K1,K2) pair:**
Enumerate all (K1,K2) ∈ V×V (2^40 combos)? Too many. 

**Hash join on U and X:** 
Condition K1∈V ⇔ X ∈ A_0 ⊕ F(U⊕V) (call set S1(U), size 2^20).
Condition K2∈V ⇔ X ∈ F^{-1}(U⊕R3_0) ⊕ V (call set S2(U), size 2^20).

For each U (2^20), we want X ∈ S1(U)∩S2(U)∩S3 where S3 = {R4_0⊕F(R3_0⊕v) : v∈V}.

We can precompute S3 as a hash set (2^20 entries; membership O(1)).
For a given U, S1(U) = {A_0⊕F(U⊕v)} — testing whether a given X is in S1(U) requires checking if U ∈ F^{-1}(X⊕A_0)⊕V (hash lookup on V). S2(U) = {w(U)⊕v : v∈V} where w(U)=F^{-1}(U⊕R3_0); membership of X in S2(U) ⇔ X⊕w(U) ∈ V (hash lookup on V).

So for each U (2^20), iterate over X ∈ S3 (2^20) and do two hash lookups → 2^40. As before.

To avoid iterating over all X∈S3 for each U: Note S1(U) has the form A_0⊕F(U⊕V). For each X ∈ S3, the condition U ∈ F^{-1}(X⊕A_0)⊕V gives a SET of U's of size 2^20 (a V-coset). So for each X∈S3 (2^20), the U's that satisfy K1∈V are U ∈ F^{-1}(X⊕A_0)⊕V = a coset of V... wait, V is not a subgroup, so F^{-1}(X⊕A_0)⊕V is just a set of size 2^20.

So: For each X ∈ S3 (2^20), we need to find U ∈ C1(X) := F^{-1}(X⊕A_0)⊕V such that K2∈V holds: X ∈ S2(U) ⇔ X⊕F^{-1}(U⊕R3_0) ∈ V. 

Now iterate over the U-space (2^20) for each X → 2^40 again.

Alternatively note that both C1(X) and the K2 condition involve "⊕V" cosets. If V were a subgroup, we could compress cosets. But V isn't a subgroup.

Hmm, let me check: is V = image(expand | 20-bit) actually a subgroup or affine subspace? Let me test expand for actual GF(2)-linearity. expand(s) = s ⊕ (s<<16) ⊕ (s*12345). The s*12345 term: 12345 = 0x3039. Multiplication by 12345 mod 2^32 is NOT a GF(2)-linear map because multiplication by a constant integer involves carries... BUT over GF(2), multiplication by a constant can be represented as a linear map! In GF(2)^32, multiplication by a constant c corresponds to a linear transformation? Actually, multiplication by c in the ring Z/2^32 is NOT GF(2)-linear because carry propagation makes it nonlinear. E.g., 2*0x80000000 = 0 (mod 2^32) but 2*(0x7FF) ... carries make it not linear. So V is not a subspace.

**Cutting down with a hash-join to ~2^28:**

- X-values: 2^20 (from K3); store in array with their K3.

Construct two hash sets:
- HashSetV for membership in V.
- We want pairs. This is a **sparse matrix join**. A standard approach: for each U (2^20), iterate over v∈V (2^20): X = v ⊕ W1(U); then check if X is in X-set (hash) AND W2(X)⊕U ∈ V. That iteration is 2^40.

Alternative: for each X (2^20), iterate over v∈V (2^20): U = v ⊕ W2(X); check if U in U-set and W1(U)⊕X ∈ V → 2^40.

Neither helps without further structure.

Let me look at this from a "linear attack" reference I now recall more concretely — I think the FEAL linear attack uses **16-bit chunks of the round keys** and a **2^16 brute force per chunk**, solving 4×2^16 = 2^18 total (for FEAL-8 with 32-bit keys). The key trick is that the differential lets you peel the last round in *16-bit chunks* independently. For our 20-bit seeds (which expand to 32-bit keys), maybe a similar chunk-wise attack applies.

Let me try to reconstruct: The last round equation R2 = R4 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3). The F function operates byte-wise with dependencies y2→y3,y1→y0. The *second half* of the F output (bytes 0,1: y0,y1) depends on x0,x1 and y1/y2. Due to the differential property, maybe we can recover K3 *byte-pair* (bytes 0,1) and (bytes 2,3) separately with 2^16 each.

Recall the exact differential: B=0x80800000 flips bytes 2,3 (input) → output flips byte 3 bit1. A=0x00008080 flips bytes 0,1 → output flips byte 0 bit1. These two masks affect *disjoint* output bytes (byte3 vs byte0) and depend on *disjoint* input bytes (2,3 vs 0,1)! This suggests the F function is *separable into two halves*:
- The "high" part {input bytes 2,3} affects output bytes 2,3 (specifically byte 3) in a linear-like way.
- The "low" part {input bytes 0,1} affects output bytes 0,1 (specifically byte 0) in a linear-like way.

Actually looking at F more carefully:
y2 = rol2[(x3^x2) + (x1^x0) + 1]
y3 = rol2[x3 + y2]
y1 = rol2[(x1^x0) + y2]
y0 = rol2[x0 + y1 + 1]

There's coupling: y2 depends on both halves, and feeds into y3 and y1. So not fully separable, but the *differential* properties focus on the top bits.

OK let me try yet another angle: **linear algebra over the pairs to solve for keys without search**, using the fact that we have 32 pairs and the F inverse formulas:

For each pair j, define the derived keys in terms of the unknown intermediate states R1_j, R2_j:
K0 = F^{-1}(L0_j⊕R1_j)⊕A_j
K1 = F^{-1}(A_j⊕R2_j)⊕R1_j
K2 = F^{-1}(R1_j⊕R3_j)⊕R2_j
K3 = F^{-1}(R2_j⊕R4_j)⊕R3_j

The K's are the same across all j. So we have, for each j, a relation:
F^{-1}(L0_j⊕R1_j)⊕A_j = F^{-1}(L0_0⊕R1_0)⊕A_0   [K0 match]
etc.

If we GUESS the actual key value K0 (2^20), then for each j: R1_j = L0_j ⊕ F(K0⊕A_j) (determined). Then K1,K2,K3 consistency gives equations for R2_j:
K1 = F^{-1}(A_j⊕R2_j)⊕R1_j → R2_j = A_j ⊕ F(K1⊕R1_j)   [K1 determines R2_j]
K2 = F^{-1}(R1_j⊕R3_j)⊕R2_j → R2_j = F^{-1}(R1_j⊕R3_j)⊕K2   [K2 determines R2_j]
K3 = F^{-1}(R2_j⊕R4_j)⊕R3_j → R2_j = F(K3⊕R3_j)⊕R4_j   [K3 determines R2_j]

So for fixed K0, each of K1,K2,K3 gives R2_j as a function of j and the key. Consistency: the R2_j from K1 must equal that from K2 and K3 for all j. That's exactly the earlier system. 

Now, here's a NEW idea: use R2_j consistency between K1 and K2:
A_j ⊕ F(K1⊕R1_j) = F^{-1}(R1_j⊕R3_j) ⊕ K2   for all j.
⇒ F(K1⊕R1_j) ⊕ K2 = A_j ⊕ F^{-1}(R1_j⊕R3_j) = A_j ⊕ M_j = N_j.
This is Eq B. For two pairs, eliminates K2. We need to find (K0,K1) such that:
F(K1⊕R1_j) ⊕ F(K1⊕R1_k) = N_j ⊕ N_k for all j,k, with R1_j,N_j from K0.

This is a constraint on (K0,K1). We could MITM over K0 and K1 with hashing:
- For pair 0 and each K0: R1_0, N_0. For each K1: compute F(K1⊕R1_0).
- For pair 1: R1_1,N_1. For each K1: compute F(K1⊕R1_1).
- We need F(K1⊕R1_0)⊕F(K1⊕R1_1) = N_0⊕N_1.

Still couples K0 and K1 (through R1). No clean split.

Alright, I think I need to actually look up the FEAL "linear attack" — let me try to recall it via the *name*: **"linear attack recovers round-keys"**. 

OH WAIT. I think I've got it now. The "linear attack" on FEAL is indeed the following:

Because F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ 0x80800000) = 0x02000000 with probability 1, the ciphertext difference for two plaintexts differing by a specific mask is *known*, independent of key. This lets the attacker compute the **R2 value** for chosen plaintexts. Specifically:

Take two plaintexts P and P' where P' differs from P by a mask **Δ** such that after the first two rounds, the R2 values differ by a *known* amount (which may involve K0,K1 through the linear differential). Then by choosing Δ appropriately, the attacker gets equations that determine K0 and K1.

But for known-plaintext attack with random pairs...

For each known pair (P,C), and for each candidate value of *the XOR of certain key bytes*, compute a quantity and count how many pairs match the linear equation. The exact differential gives equations that hold with *probability 1* for *any* plaintext, when you consider **XOR differences between the F outputs in successive rounds**...

Let me think about the "linear attack" from the FEAL paper once more — maybe it reduces the search to per-key 2^16 or 2^8 chunks. Let me try to actually recall the differential analysis with the *separability* of F.

**F's separability and byte-pair key recovery:**

Look at F again:
y2 = g(x3^x2, x1^x0, 1)
y3 = g(x3, y2, 0)
y1 = g(x1^x0, y2, 0)
y0 = g(x0, y1, 1)

If we consider the LAST round and want to recover K3, the input to F is R3 ⊕ K3. The output affects R2 via R4 = R2 ⊕ y. If we could determine *part* of the F output (say bytes 0,1) from the equation R2_j = A_j ⊕ F(R1_j⊕K1)... this couples with K1.

The exact differentials being separable (mask A affects only byte 0 output; mask B affects only byte 3 output) suggests an attack on *bytes*.

Let me search for *byte-level* exact differentials or linear relations. I already found:
- F(x)⊕F(x⊕0x00008080) = 0x00000002 (only byte 0 changes, low bit)
- F(x)⊕F(x⊕0x80800000) = 0x02000000 (only byte 3 changes)

These involve *pairs of bytes* (0,1) and (2,3). This suggests recovering K3 bytes (0,1) and (2,3) separately with 2^16 each.

The idea: To recover the LOW half of K3 (bytes 0,1), use the differential A=0x00008080 which affects only output byte 0. Feed inputs that differ by A into F, output differs only in byte 0 (by 0x02). The HIGH half (bytes 2,3) is unaffected. This lets us decouple the halves!

Let me try to build a byte-wise attack for the last round:

We want K3. Consider the *low half* (bytes 0,1) of K3: call LK3 = K3 & 0x0000FFFF. The high half HK3 = K3 & 0xFFFF0000.

The F input x = R3 ⊕ K3. The low bytes of x depend on LK3 and low bytes of R3; high bytes depend on HK3 and high bytes of R3.

From F's structure, the output bytes y0,y1 (low) depend on low input bytes x0,x1 and on y2 (which depends on all bytes). The output byte y2 and y3 (high) depend on all bytes too. So not clean separation.

BUT the differential A=0x00008080 (flip low bytes 0,1) changes ONLY output byte 0 (by 0x02) — the high output bytes stay the same. So the *high* output bytes are *insensitive* to the low input bytes in a specific way. This could allow recovering high/low halves.

I think the actual FEAL linear attack recovers the key bytes using the following trick per round:

For the last round: define the "linear" relation on the *input top bits*. For a guess of LK3 (2^16), you can compute R2's low half from the equation (assuming HK3 correct later)...

Actually, wait. Before that, let me reconsider the search reduction with the hash-join once more. I claimed 2^40 is inherent, but let me re-examine the hash-join possibility for finding (K0,K3) with K1,K2 valid:

We have:
- U-list: 2^20 entries (K0 → U)
- X-list: 2^20 entries (K3 → X)

We need pairs (U,X) with:
(a) U ⊕ W2(X) ∈ V  where W2(X)=F^{-1}(X⊕A_0)
(b) X ⊕ W1(U) ∈ V  where W1(U)=F^{-1}(U⊕R3_0)

This is a **bipartite join** with two membership constraints. It can be solved if we can index by cosets, but V isn't a subgroup.

However, here's a different idea: **represent V as a hash set and use a "nested loop with early termination" but on a REDUCED U-set**. Wait — expected valid pairs = 2^16. We can find them by first satisfying condition (a) only: pairs with U⊕W2(X)∈V. Then filter by (b). The set of (U,X) satisfying (a): For each X (2^20), U ∈ W2(X)⊕V (a set of size 2^20). So total pairs satisfying (a) = 2^20×2^20×2^-12 = 2^28. We can enumerate these 2^28 pairs efficiently:

For each X∈X-list (2^20):
  compute W = W2(X)
  for each v∈V (2^20):
    U = W⊕v
    if U in U-set (hash): we have a candidate (U,X), check condition (b).
This is 2^20×2^20 = 2^40 again (iterating v for each X). 

But we can **swap**: For each X (2^20), we need U ∈ (W⊕V) ∩ U-set. Instead of iterating all v∈V, note W⊕V is a set of size 2^20; intersecting with U-set (size 2^20) in a 2^32 universe. Expected intersection size = 2^20×2^20/2^32 = 2^8. To find the intersection efficiently: build a hash set of U-values (2^20). Then for the set W⊕V, iterate over v∈V (2^20) and look up U=W⊕v in U-hash → 2^20 lookups per X. Total 2^40.

The intersection of two random sets of size 2^20 in a 2^32 universe, output size 2^8, CAN be computed faster than 2^20 per X using **sorting**? The set W⊕V is a "shifted V" — but V isn't structured. Actually — we can precompute a hash table over ALL v∈V (2^20 entries). For each X, we want U ∈ (W⊕V) ∩ U-set. Equivalently find u∈U-set such that u⊕W ∈ V. For each u∈U-set (2^20), check (u⊕W)∈V via hash lookup. That's 2^20 per X → 2^40.

Hmm, the fundamental issue: joining two sets of size 2^20 each with a membership predicate that depends on a "shift" (⊕W where W varies per element) costs 2^20 per element of one side → 2^40 total.

The only way to reduce is if V has structure (subgroup) allowing coset compression. V = image of expand. Let me check whether expand restricted to 20-bit seeds has a "nice" structure. expand(s) = s ^ (s<<16) ^ (s*12345). For 20-bit s, s<<16 is nonzero only for bits 0..3 (since s<2^20, s<<16 < 2^36). The 32-bit value is s ^ (s<<16) ^ (s*12345) mod 2^32. The term s*12345 involves carries. This is a pseudorandom-ish injection from 20 to 32 bits. Not a subgroup.

**Algorithm (C, OpenMP):**

Precompute:
- pairs: for each of 32 pairs, store A_j, L0_j, R3_j, R4_j.
- V_bitset: bitset[1<<32] = 512MB. For each seed s<2^20, set bit expand(s). (Use uint8_t array of size 2^32 or use bitset of 2^29 bytes? A bit per value: 2^32 bits = 512MB. Or use a byte per value: 2^32 bytes = 4GB — also fine with 127GB RAM but slower w.r.t. cache. Let's use bitset (bit per value): 2^32 bits = 512 MB.)
- For pair 0 (use pair index 0 as the "probe pair"): precompute for each K3 (s3 seeds): X[s3] = R4_0 ^ f(R3_0 ^ expand(s3)); W2[s3] = f_inv(A_0 ^ X[s3]). This is 2^20 arrays (4MB each). 
- Also precompute for each K0 (s0 seeds): U[s0] = L0_0 ^ f(A_0 ^ expand(s0)); W1[s0] = f_inv(U[s0] ^ R3_0). 2^20 arrays.

Then the main loop over s0 (parallized):
  U = U[s0]; W1v = W1[s0];
  for s3 in 0..2^20-1:
    K1 = W2[s3] ^ U;
    if (bitset[K1]) {
      K2 = W1v ^ X[s3];
      if (bitset[K2]) {
        // candidate found: (K0=expand(s0), K2? wait we derived K1,K2 and K3=expand(s3), K0=expand(s0))
        // verify across all 32 pairs
        ...
      }
    }

Wait, I need to double check the equations. Let me carefully derive the K1,K2 formulas in terms of pair 0 data.

Given K0 (s0) and K3 (s3) for pair 0:
- A = A_0 = R0_0 ⊕ L0_0 (plaintext derived)
- U = R1_0 = L0_0 ⊕ F(A ⊕ K0)  ✓
- X = R2_0 = R4_0 ⊕ F(R3_0 ⊕ K3) ✓ (from round 4: R4 = R2 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3) ⇒ R2 = R4 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3))

Round 2: R2 = A ⊕ F(R1 ⊕ K1) ⇒ F(R1⊕K1) = A ⊕ R2 = A ⊕ X ⇒ K1 = F^{-1}(A ⊕ X) ⊕ R1 = F^{-1}(A⊕X) ⊕ U. ✓ (So W2 = F^{-1}(A_0 ⊕ X).)

Round 3: R3 = R1 ⊕ F(R2 ⊕ K2) ⇒ F(R2⊕K2) = R1 ⊕ R3 = U ⊕ R3_0 ⇒ K2 = F^{-1}(U ⊕ R3_0) ⊕ R2 = F^{-1}(U ⊕ R3_0) ⊕ X. ✓ (So W1 = F^{-1}(U ⊕ R3_0).)

Great, so formula:
K1 = F^{-1}(A_0 ⊕ X) ⊕ U

[tool-d2b] read_file ->     38	// FEAL G function
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     39	uint8_t g_function(uint8_t a, uint8_t b, uint8_t x) {
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     40	    uint8_t temp = (a + b + x) & 0xFF;  // Keep it as 8-bit
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     41	    return ((temp << 2) | (temp >> 6)) & 0xFF;
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     42	}
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     43	
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     44	// FEAL F function
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     45	uint32_t f_function(uint32_t input_val) {
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     46	    uint8_t x[4];
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     47	    uint8_t y[4];
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     48	    
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     49	    // Extract bytes from input (big-endian)
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     50	    for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     51	        x[i] = input_val & 0xFF;
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     52	        input_val >>= 8;
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     53	    }
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     54	    
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     55	    // Apply G function operations
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     56	    y[2] = g_function(x[3] ^ x[2], x[1] ^ x[0], 1);
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     57	    y[3] = g_function(x[3], y[2], 0);
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     58	    y[1] = g_function(x[1] ^ x[0], y[2], 0);
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     59	    y[0] = g_function(x[0], y[1], 1);
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     60	    
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     61	    // Combine bytes into output (big-endian)
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     62	    uint32_t output = 0;
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     63	    for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     64	        output |= (uint32_t)y[i] << (8 * i);
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     65	    }
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     66	    
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     67	    return output & 0xFFFFFFFF;
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     68	}
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     69	
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     70	
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     71	// FEAL decryption function
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     72	uint64_t decrypt(uint64_t ciphertext) {
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     73	    // The encryption ends with: return merge(right, left)
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     74	    // So extract in swapped order
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     75	    uint32_t left = getright(ciphertext);   // What was stored as right
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     76	    uint32_t right = getleft(ciphertext);   // What was stored as left
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     77	    
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     78	    // Reverse post-round mixing: left ^= right
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     79	    left ^= right;
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     80	    
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     81	    // Reverse 4 rounds of Feistel network (in reverse order)
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     82	    for (int round_num = 3; round_num >= 0; round_num--) {
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     83	        // In encryption: right = left ^ mixed, left = temp (old right)
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     84	        // To reverse: restore old values
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     85	        uint32_t temp = left;
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     86	        left = right;
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     87	        uint32_t mixed = f_function(temp ^ key[round_num]);
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     88	        right = temp;
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     89	        left ^= mixed;
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     90	    }
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     91	    
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     92	    // Reverse pre-round mixing: right ^= left
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     93	    right ^= left;
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     94	    
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     95	    // Combine in normal order
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     96	    return merge(left, right);
[tool-d2b] read_file ->     97	}

[tool-a38] read_file ->     30	    v = input_val & 0xFFFFFFFF

[penguin] output cap clamped hard: max_tokens 256000 -> 111270 (context_window 128000, estimated input 15706)
Now I have a good understanding of the task. Let me think about the attack strategy.

The cipher:
- 64-bit block split into left/right halves
- Pre-mix: right ^= left
- 4 Feistel rounds: mixed = f_function(right ^ key[i]); right = left ^ mixed; left = temp(old right)
- Post: left ^= right; output = merge(right, left)

So encrypt(plaintext):
- left0 = PL, right0 = PR
- right0 ^= left0 => right_pre = PR ^ PL
- for r in 0..3:
  - mixed = f(right ^ key[r])
  - temp = right
  - right = left ^ mixed
  - left = temp
- left ^= right
- return merge(right, left)

Let me trace through:
- L0 = PL, R0 = PR
- Pre: R0 = PR ^ PL
- Round 0: L1 = R0, R1 = L0 ^ f(R0 ^ K0)
- Round 1: L2 = R1, R2 = L1 ^ f(R1 ^ K1)
- Round 2: L3 = R2, R3 = L2 ^ f(R2 ^ K2)
- Round 3: L4 = R3, R4 = L3 ^ f(R3 ^ K3)
- Post: L4 ^= R4, so L4' = L4 ^ R4
- Output: merge(R4, L4') => CL = R4, CR = L4'

From ciphertext: CL = R4, CR = L4' = L4 ^ R4. So R3 = L4 = CL ^ CR.

So known from ciphertext: R4 = CL, R3 = CL ^ CR.

Now from plaintext and decryption:

ΔL0 = 0x02000000? Let me recall the Matsui attack on FEAL: the approximation over round function F is:
F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ ΔX) = ΔY
with ΔX = 0x80800000, ΔY = 0x02000000.

And the linear approximation for a single round:
ΔL_{r+1} = ΔR_r (from L_{r+1}=R_r)
ΔR_{r+1} = ΔL_r ⊕ ΔY if ΔR_r = ΔX.

This is used to construct a 2- or 3-round linear approximation between plaintext and ciphertext. Then, using the approximation, you can recover the last round key K by checking, for each candidate key, whether the approximation holds (count biased).

So:

Round 3: F(R3 ^ K3) = R2 ^ R4 → K3 = R3 ^ F^{-1}(R2 ^ R4).

So if we know R1 and R2 (which are internal), we can immediately compute K2 and K3.

But R1 and R2 come from round 0 and 1:
R1 = PL ^ F(R0 ^ K0) — if we know K0, we get R1.
R2 = R0 ^ F(R1 ^ K1) — if we know K1 too, we get R2.

So this connects: guess K0 and K1 (2^40), compute R1, R2, then derive K2, K3 from the known-relations (rounds 2 and 3). Actually we don't even need to derive; we compute K2 = R2 ^ F^{-1}(R1 ^ R3) (per-pair) and K3 = R3 ^ F^{-1}(R2 ^ R4) (per-pair). If consistent across pairs, we have keys.

So the search is over (K0, K1) → 2^40, or over (K0, K3) → 2^40 (from the earlier derivation). Either way, 2^40 is too much.

Let me think about the meet-in-the-middle between K0 and K1.

For each pair i:
R1_i = PL_i ^ F(PR_i ^ PL_i ^ K0)  [depends on K0]
R2_i = (PR_i ^ PL_i) ^ F(R1_i ^ K1)  [depends on K0, K1]
R3_i = R1_i ^ F(R2_i ^ K2)  [known R3]
R4_i = R2_i ^ F(R3_i ^ K3)  [known R4]

From R3_i: F(R2_i ^ K2) = R1_i ^ R3_i.
From R4_i: F(R3_i ^ K3) = R2_i ^ R4_i.

So K2 and K3 are determined by R1_i, R2_i (and known R3_i, R4_i):
K2_i = R2_i ^ F^{-1}(R1_i ^ R3_i)
K3_i = R3_i ^ F^{-1}(R2_i ^ R4_i)

Given (K0, K1) we compute R1_i, R2_i for all pairs, derive candidate K2_i, K3_i for each pair, and check consistency across pairsholidays. If all pairs give the same K2 and K3, we have a full key.

F-affine representation: Let me define the linearized F. From the differential relations:

Actually, let me derive the full linearized representation of F. The relations:
F(x ^ 0x00008080) ^ F(x) = 0x00000002
F(x ^ 0x80800000) ^ F(x) = 0x02000000
F(x ^ 0x80808080) ^ F(x) = 0x02000002

These are linear structures: for a ∈ {0x00008080, 0x80800000}, F(x)^F(x^a) = const. This means that F is affine when restricted to any coset of the subspace spanned by a1, a2. But F is otherwise highly nonlinear.

We have, for each pair i:
(eq1) R1_i ^ F(R0_i ^ K0) = PL_i  → equivalent to R1_i = PL_i ^ F(R0_i ^ K0)
(eq2) R2_i ^ F(R1_i ^ K1) = R0_i  → R2_i = R0_i ^ F(R1_i ^ K1)
(eq3) R3_i ^ F(R2_i ^ K2) = R1_i  → R3_i = R1_i ^ F(R2_i ^ K2)
(eq4) R4_i ^ F(R3_i ^ K3) = R2_i  → R4_i = R2_i ^ F(R3_i ^ K3)

Unknowns: R1_i, R2_i (per pair), K0..K3.

Since F is a bijection:
K0 = R0_i ^ F^{-1}(R1_i ^ PL_i)  [from eq1: F(R0_i ^ K0) = R1_i ^ PL_i]
K1 = R1_i ^ F^{-1}(R2_i ^ R0_i)
K2 = R2_i ^ F^{-1}(R3_i ^ R1_i)
K3 = R3_i ^ F^{-1}(R4_i ^ R2_i)

All four key equations must give the same K for all pairs i.

So we have unknowns R1_i, R2_i per pair (64 unknowns for 32 pairs) plus 4 keys. We have 4 equations per pair = 128 equations. Highly overdetermined.

Wait, but maybe global relations DO hold but only with the right key-dependent constants — i.e., affine relations with constant determined by key. The classic FEAL linear attack computes:

For the cipher, with the linear approximation F(x) ≈ L(x) ^ c where L is linear:
C = L'(P) ^ KEY' for some linear function L' and key-dependent constant.

Specifically, since F is "approximately" affine with L(x) being linear (the linearization replacing byte add with XOR), the full cipher becomes approximately affine: C ≈ A(P) ^ B(K). Then from many pairs you can recover B(K) or parts of it via correlation.

This maps (u, R0) → (R3, v)? Round 1: input left=u? wait. Let me re-map. Actual rounds:
Round 1: L2 = R1. Hmm let me re-index the 2-round core.

We have (from the full cipher):
- After round 1 and 2, states: L2, R2, L3, R3.

Round 1: L2 = R1, R2 = L1 ^ F(R1 ^ K1)
Round 2: L3 = R2, R3 = L2 ^ F(R2 ^ K2)

With L1 = R0, R1 = u. So:
- L2 = u
- R2 = R0 ^ F(u ^ K1)
- L3 = R2 (= v)
- R3 = L2 ^ F(R2 ^ K2) = u ^ F(v ^ K2)

So indeed the 2-round core is: given (u, R0), compute R2 = R0 ^ F(u^K1) = v and R3 = u ^ F(v^K2).

In reverse: given (R3, v), compute u = R3 ^ F(v ^ K2) and R0 = v ^ F(u ^ K1).

So if we guess K3 to get v (R2), the remaining problem resembles a 2-round cipher with keys K1, K2 and known input R0 (from plaintext) and output R3 (from ciphertext). The unknown is u and keys K1, K2.

To break a 2-round Feistel: guess K2 (20 bits), compute u_i = R3_i ^ F(v_i ^ K2). Then check consistency of K1 via R0_i = v_i ^ F(u_i ^ K1) → K1 = u_i ^ F^{-1}(v_i ^ R0_i). So for fixed v (via K3) and guessing K2 → compute u and then K1. That's again 2^20 (K2) per K3 → 2^40 total.

Alternatively — is there a possibility that the keys are recoverable via brute force over 2^20 if one uses the LINEAR STRUCTURE to isolate key bits? Let me look at the F function structure byte-by-byte.

F function (LSB-first bytes x0,x1,x2,x3):
- a = x3 ^ x2; b = x1 ^ x0.
- y2 = rol2(a + b + 1)
- y3 = rol2(x3 + y2)
- y1 = rol2(b + y2)
- y0 = rol2(x0 + y1 + 1)

Note y2 depends only on (a, b) = (x3^x2, x1^x0): pairs. Actually y2 depends on x3^x2 and x1^x0. y3 depends on x3 and y2. y1 depends on x1^x0 and y2. y0 depends on x0 and y1.

Inverse (from bench.c):
- x3 = inv_rol2(y3) - y2
- x1x0 = inv_rol2(y1) - y2
- x3x2 = inv_rol2(y2) - x1x0 - 1  [since y2 = rol2((x3^x2)+(x1^x0)+1)]
- x2 = x3 ^ x3x2
- x0 = inv_rol2(y0) - y1 - 1
- x1 = x0 ^ x1x0

OK. Now the byte-level structure might allow solving equations byte-by-byte with reduced complexity. But this is hand-analysis per key.

Can we reduce perf per candidate? The best check uses only 2 pairs initially: compute for pairs 0 and 1, check K1 consistency (α_0 == α_1). If mismatch, reject (probability of false accept 2^-32). So:
Per (K2,K3): 
- v_0 = R4_0 ^ F(R3_0 ^ K3)  [precomputed for K3]
- v_1 = R4_1 ^ F(R3_1 ^ K3)  [precomputed]
- u_0 = R3_0 ^ F(v_0 ^ K2); u_1 = R3_1 ^ F(v_1 ^ K2)
- α_0 = u_0 ^ F^{-1}(v_0 ^ R0_0); α_1 = u_1 ^ F^{-1}(v_1 ^ R0_1)
- if α_0 != α_1 → reject.

That's 2 F + 2 inv-F per candidate ≈ but with early exit: 2 F + 1 inv-F usually (compute α_0, α_1... need both). ~4 ops. Then 2^40 × 4 = 4.4e12 ops. Hmm OK let's think about whether 2^40 brute force with early rejection is the intended solution. The task seems like a CTF-ish problem where the solution is to implement Matsui's linear attack. Let me search my memory for the FEAL linear attack details.

The classic FEAL-4 linear attack (Matsui, "Cryptanalysis of FEAL-4 and FEAL-8"):

For FEAL-4, the attack uses the linear approximation:
ΔX = 0x80800000, ΔY = 0x02000000 (for F).

Round by round, the approximation is:
- If input to F differs by ΔX = 0x80800000, output differs by ΔY = 0x02000000 with probability 1.

The 2-round approximation: for the Feistel, a difference propagates: 
(L_i, R_i) → (L_{i+1}, R_{i+1})
ΔL_{i+1} = ΔR_i
ΔR_{i+1} = ΔL_i ⊕ ΔY (if ΔR_i = ΔX)

For FEAL-4 with pre/post whitening... The pre-mixing right ^= left and post-mixing left ^= right complicate.

Actually, the standard FEAL key schedule and the attack: The attack recovers the last subkey (K8 for FEAL-8) and then iterates. It uses a linear approximation of the last few rounds: compute, for each ciphertext and candidate last-round-key, whether the linear relation (expressed via a specific parity bit that is biased) holds. Count over many plaintext-ciphertext pairs; the correct key yields the highest bias.

In FEAL-4 with a 32-bit approximation over the last round and an 8-bit key guess, you get the key.

But here the round count is 4 and each subkey is 20 bits (not 8 per half). Matsui's attack on FEAL-4 recovers key halves (8 bits each) via counters.

The bias: In FEAL, for the full cipher, the relation between plaintext and ciphertext has a probability of 1/2 + ε for certain linear combinations gradients. This "linear hull" allows hypothesis testing of the last round key bits.

Given our F has EXACT differentials (probability 1, not 1/2+ε), the linear attack should be very strong — maybe even exact relations exist for the full cipher!

Actually, let's think: F's linear structure means the affine approximation L(x) = F(x) ⊕ F(0)... no, linear structures are different from affine approximation.

The linear structure says: for the subspace A (differences within the 4-element set), F(x) ⊕ F(x ⊕ a) = φ(a). This means the restriction of F to each coset of A is an affine shift. This is a local property, not a global affine approximation.

BUT for the differential attack: if we choose plaintext differences ΔP such that they propagate through all rounds staying in the subspaces where F's differential is exact, then the ciphertext difference is exactly determined by ΔP (independent of key!): C(P) ⊕ C(P ⊕ ΔP) = ΔC(P-independent).

Actually, here's a cleaner idea using the linear structure with all its power:

Since F(x ⊕ a) = F(x) ⊕ b for a1=0x00008080 (b1=0x00000002), a2=0x80800000 (b2=0x02000000), consider the spaces:
A = {0, a1, a2, a3}, B = {0, b1, b2, b3}.

Now consider the cipher with pre/post mixing. The input difference to round r's F is R_r ⊕ K_r. If we XOR a into the input to F (i.e., into R_r), output F changes by b.

In the Feistel: ΔR_{r+1} = ΔL_r ⊕ b if ΔR_r = a (since R_{r+1} = L_r ⊕ F(R_r⊕K_r)).

A "good" difference is where ΔL_r = 0 and ΔR_r ∈ A, then ΔR_{r+1} = b, ΔL_{r+1} = a.

Let's find a plaintext difference ΔP = (ΔL0, ΔR0) that propagates cleanly:

Pre-mix: R0 = PR ⊕ PL. So ΔR0 = ΔPR ⊕ ΔPL. L0 = PL, ΔL0 = ΔPL.

If we want ΔR0 ∈ A and ΔL0 = 0: then ΔPL = 0, ΔPR = ΔR0 ∈ A. So plaintext difference is (0, a) for a ∈ A: P ⊕ ΔP differs only in right half by a ∈ A.

Round 0: input to F is R0 ⊕ K0. ΔR0 = a ∈ A. Output F diff = b. 
ΔR1 = ΔL0 ⊕ b = b. ΔL1 = ΔR0 = a.

Round 1: input to F is R1 ⊕ K1. ΔR1 = b ∉ A (b is not in A in general). Hmm, so the "good" propagation stops unless b ∈ A. b1=0x00000002, b2=0x02000000, b3=0x02000002. A contains 0x00008080, 0x80800000, 0x80808080. So b ∉ A. So after round 0, the difference leaves the good subspace → differential becomes probabilistic (no longer exact). That's why global affine relations don't hold.

So a clean exact differential through the whole cipher only works for ≤1 round of F. That matches "no global affine relations".

OK so a pure differential attack isn't directly possible.

expand(k) = k ^ (k<<16) ^ (k*12345) with k 20-bit. The expanded key is 32 bits but only 2^20 distinct values. So each K_j ∈ S = {expand(k) : k ∈ [0, 2^20)}. This doesn't reduce the search space (still 2^20 per key), but means keys live in a set of 2^20.

The linearization isn't great. So the attack must use the exact differentials (probability 1) — those are the "linear relations" that hold exactly. The linear attack for FEAL uses these exact differentials, and the bias comes from combining them probabilistically. But with a 4-round cipherley, the exact relations at F level give rise to a linear approximation over the whole cipher with probability 1/2 + something per bit. With 32 pairs, we'd need a strong bias.

Hmm wait, but maybe the intended attack isn't via the full-cipher differential. Let me reconsider.

Given 32 known pairshare, maybe the attack is to recover the last round key by brute-forcing 2^20 K3 "votes" using a linear approximation through rounds 0-2, then peel and continue. Let me think about the standard FEAL linear attack in context of our cipher with pre/post mixing.

Standard FEAL-4 linear attack outline (from literature):
1. Choose a linear approximation (ΔL, ΔR) for a section of the cipher with probability 1/2 + ε.
2. For each candidate for the last subkey, compute the partial decryption and test the linear relation, accumulating counts.
3. The candidate with most extreme bias is the correct key.

For FEAL-4, the attack uses a 3-round approximation (rounds enne) etc. The key recovery recovers the last round key in 2^8 steps (each byte independently), then reduces the cipher and repeats.

With our 20-bit keys, exponential might be 2^20 per round key, and the bias would need ~2^(something) pairs. But we have only 32 pairs. If the bias is strong (like 2^-1), 32 pairs works.

Let me actually try to find the best linear approximation for the full cipher empirically! Since we have many such analysis possibilities hidden... hmm.

Alternatively, let me reconsider the exact differentials from the standpoint of the whole cipher. The exact differentials of F:
ΔF for Δx ∈ A = {0x00008080, 0x80800000, 0x80808080}.

Now consider the FULL cipher as a function of plaintext. The ciphertext is:
CL = R4 = R2 ⊕ F(R3 ⊕ K3)
CR = L4 ⊕ R4 = R3 ⊕ R4

We know R3 = CL ⊕ CR and R4 = CL.

Now here's a thought for recovering K3: For a guessed K3, compute:
R2 = R4 ⊕ F(R3 ⊕ K3).
Now R2 should equal the internal R2 from the encryption. R2 = R0 ⊕ F(R1 ⊕ K1). Not directly verifiable without more keys...

But here's a KEY structural observation: R2 satisfying both eq2 and eq4. Let me think of using two pairs: R2_i = R0_i ⊕ F(R1_i ⊕ K1) and R2_i = R4_i ⊕ F(R3_i ⊕ K3).

Actually — different idea. What about a chosen-KEY-free algebraic angle: Use the exact differential to find relations between pairs that allow key recovery by solving for K3 using only pairs (P,C).

Let me think about treating the whole encryption as:
CL = encrypt_L(P), CR = encrypt_R(P).

Round equations reorganized using F^{-1}:
K0 = R0 ⊕ F^{-1}(PL ⊕ R1)
K1 = R1 ⊕ F^{-1}(R0 ⊕ R2)
K2 = R2 ⊕ F^{-1}(R1 ⊕ R3)
K3 = R3 ⊕ F^{-1}(R2 ⊕ R4)

(verify: from R1 = PL ⊕ F(R0 ⊕ K0) → F(R0⊕K0) = PL ⊕ R1 → R0⊕K0 = F^{-1}(PL ⊕ R1) → K0 = R0 ⊕ F^{-1}(PL⊕R1). Yes.)
(Similarly: R2 = R0 ⊕ F(R1⊕K1) → F(R1⊕K1) = R0⊕R2 → K1 = R1 ⊕ F^{-1}(R0⊕R2). Yes.)
(R3 = R1 ⊕ F(R2⊕K2) → K2 = R2 ⊕ F^{-1}(R1⊕R3). Yes.)
(R4 = R2 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3) → K3 = R3 ⊕ F^{-1}(R2⊕R4). Yes.)

Since K0..K3 are the same across pairs:
For all i, j:
R0_i ⊕ F^{-1}(PL_i ⊕ R1_i) = R0_j ⊕ F^{-1}(PL_j ⊕ R1_j)  [K0 equality]
R1_i ⊕ F^{-1}(R0_i ⊕ R2_i) = R1_j ⊕ F^{-1}(R0_j ⊕ R2_j)  [K1]
R2_i ⊕ F^{-1}(R1_i ⊕ R3_i) = R2_j ⊕ F^{-1}(R1_j ⊕ R3_j)  [K2]
R3_i ⊕ F^{-1}(R2_i ⊕ R4_i) = R3_j ⊕ F^{-1}(R2_j ⊕ R4_j)  [K3]

Now, here's a neat trick: use the linear structures of F! F^{-1} satisfies: F^{-1}(y ⊕ b) = F^{-1}(y) ⊕ a whenever b = φ(a). I.e., F^{-1}(y ⊕ 0x00000002) = F^{-1}(y) ⊕ 0x00008080, etc.

NEW IDEA: Use the K0-equality and K3-equality equations to solve for R1 and R2 independently of K1, K2!

K0 equality: For all pairs i, R0_i ⊕ F^{-1}(PL_i ⊕ R1_i) = K0 (constant). So R1_i is constrained: F^{-1}(PL_i ⊕ R1_i) = R0_i ⊕ K0 → R1_i = PL_i ⊕ F(R0_i ⊕ K0). This is what we had: R1_i determined by K0.

K3 equality: For all pairs i, R3_i ⊕ F^{-1}(R2_i ⊕ R4_i) = K3 (constant) → R2_i = R4_i ⊕ F(R3_i ⊕ K3). R2_i determined by K3.

So R1 = f(K0), R2 = f(K3). Now K1 and K2 must satisfy:
R1_i ⊕ F^{-1}(R0_i ⊕ R2_i) = K1  for all i
R2_i ⊕ F^{-1}(R1_i ⊕ R3_i) = K2  for all i

So the problem reduces to: find (K0, K3) such that the resulting (R1 vector, R2 vector) satisfy the two above consistency conditions. Still 2^40.

Hmm, but maybe we can split: The K1 condition involves only (R1, R2, R0) and K2 involves (R2, R1, R3). Both link R1 and R2 from the SAME (K0, K3). No separation.

K1 condition between pair 0 and 1:
R1_0 ⊕ F^{-1}(R0_0 ⊕ R2_0) = R1_1 ⊕ F^{-1}(R0_1 ⊕ R2_1)

That's one 32-bit equation in unknowns K0 (through R1_0, R1_1) and K3 (through R2_0, R2_1). With random K0,K3, this equation holds with prob 2^-32. So knowing K0_guess: for each K0 we can compute R1_0, R1_1 dove; then the equation becomes a condition on K3:
F^{-1}(R0_0 ⊕ R2_0) = R1_0 ⊕ R1_1 ⊕ F^{-1}(R0_1 ⊕ R2_1)
