Actually, let me test the hypothesis differently — maybe the keys are the 20-bit integers 0..3 or small values. Let me just try to recover the keys using general brute force over independent keys but using the algebraic structure to make it feasible.

Let me reconsider the 2^40 approach. Actually, let me reconsider — maybe there's an even smarter reduction.

Let me revisit: we have the reverse equations:
K0 = R0_i ⊕ F^{-1}(PL_i ⊕ R1_i)
K3 = R3_i ⊕ F^{-1}(R2_i ⊕ R4_i)
K1 = R1_i ⊕ F^{-1}(R0_i ⊕ R2_i)
K2 = R2_i ⊕ F^{-1}(R1_i ⊕ R3_i)

Now consider the KEY PROPERTY: K0, K1, K2, K3 are 20-bit (after expand they're specific 32-bit values). Actually, the expanded keys are 32-bit values but only 2^20 distinct, all in the image of expand. But for deriving, we can treat them as 32-bit; the final check will ensure they're valid expand outputs.

Hmm. Let me think about meeting in the middle more cleverly.

Suppose we enumerate K0 (2^20) and K3 (2^20) separately, and for each compute:
- R1 vector (from K0): r1_i = PL_i ⊕ F(R0_i ⊕ K0)
- R2 vector (from K3): r2_i = R4_i ⊕ F(R3_i ⊕ K3)

Now the consistency requires:
K1 = r1_i ⊕ F^{-1}(r2_i ⊕ R0_i) constant in i.
K2 = r2_i ⊕ F^{-1}(r1_i ⊕ R3_i) constant in i.

We need to find (K0, K3) satisfying both. The naive check is 2^40. But note: we can precompute for each K0 a "signature" and for each K3 a "signature" that must combine? The constraint couples r1_i and r2_i in a per-pair manner, then requires cross-pair equality. It's like a "matrix completion" problem. Hard to hash-join directly.

BUT, maybe we can use per-pair precomputation and a "consistent-key check via hashing" as follows:

For each candidate (K0, K3), we compute K1 candidate for pair 0 and pair 1, and if equal, proceed. Still O(2^40).

Is there a way to reduce to O(2^20) via meet-in-the-middle? Let me think about the K1 and K2 conditions as two equations A(K0, K3) = 0 and B(K0, K3) = 0. We need to find pairs (K0, K3) satisfying both. Generic problem: given two functions on (K0,K3), find common positives. No inherent MITM unless functions factor.

Let me examine if A and B factor:
K1 condition: f^{-1}(r2_i ⊕ R0_i) ⊕ r1_i = const. This mixes r1(K0) and r2(K3) per-pair. Since the "⊕ r1_i" and "⊕ const" are separations? Define h_i(K3) = F^{-1}(R4_i ⊕ F(R3_i ⊕ K3) ⊕ R0_i). Then condition: h_i(K3) ⊕ r1_i(K0) = K1 for all i.

So for a given K0, we need K3 such that h_i(K3) ⊕ r1_i(K0) is the same for all i.

Its "cross-pair difference" condition: for all i,j, h_i(K3) ⊕ h_j(K3) = r1_i ⊕ r1_j (both sides constant in K1). So K3 must satisfy: (h_i ⊕ h_j)(K3) = (r1_i ⊕ r1_j) for all pairs (i,j).

Similarly K2 condition: g_i(K0) ⊕ r2_i(K3) = K2 where g_i(K0) = F^{-1}(r1_i ⊕ R3_i), giving (g_i ⊕ g_j)(K0) = (r2_i ⊕ r2_j).

So:
K3 must satisfy, for all pairs (i,j): F^{-1}(R4_i⊕F(R3_i⊕K3)⊕R0_i) ⊕ F^{-1}(R4_j⊕F(R3_j⊕K3)⊕R0_j) = r1_i(K0) ⊕ r1_j(K0).
Tied between K3 and K0 via r1. Still coupled.

Hmm. But maybe we can structure a MITM: Suppose we enumerate all K3 (2^20). For each, compute the 32-vector of h_i = F^{-1}(R4_i ⊕ F(R3_i ⊕ K3) ⊕ R0_i). Now, for this K3 to be consistent with SOME K0, we need to find K0 such that h_i(K3) ⊕ r1_i(K0) is constant across iholiday.

Equivalently define cross-diff vector: h_i ⊕ h_0 for all i. For consistency, r1_i(K0) ⊕ r1_0(K0) must equal h_i ⊕ h_0 for all i.

Now, r1_i(K0) ⊕ r1_0(K0) = PL_i ⊕ F(R0_i⊕K0) ⊕ PL_0 ⊕ F(R0_0⊕K0).

So the condition is: F(R0_i⊕K0) ⊕ F(R0_0⊕K0) = (h_i ⊕ h_0) ⊕ (PL_i ⊕ PL_0).

This is a condition relating K0 (through the left side) to known right side. For the true K0 and true K3 this holds. But to FIND it, we can precompute for each K0 the cross-diff vector Δ_i(K0) = F(R0_i⊕K0) ⊕ F(R0_0⊕K0), and for each K3 the cross-diff vector Δ_i'(K3) = ... wait, h_i itself is known for each K3. Then for each (K0,K3), the check is Δ_i(K0) == target_i(K3) for all i, where target_i(K3) = (h_i(K3) ⊕ h_0(K3)) ⊕ (PL_i ⊕ PL_0).

So we're matching, for all i simultaneously. This is a multi-set join: find (K0, K3) pairs such that the 31-dim vector Δ(K0) equals vec(K3).

We can do this with hashing using a few pairs: For each K3, compute the target vector for pairs (1..m). For each K0, compute Δ vector. Look up K0 in hash table keyed by target vector. But the standard MITM would require precomputing tables for K0 and K3 of size 2^20 each — that's feasible!

Actually, let's formalize. We want to find (K0, K3) such that for ALL i (or at least a good subset), Δ_i(K0) = T_i(K3), where:
- Δ_i(K0) = F(R0_i ⊕ K0) ⊕ F(R0_0 ⊕ K0) ⊕ PL_i ⊕ PL_0  [combining r1 cross-diff and PL terms]
- T_i(K3) = h_i(K3) ⊕ h_0(K3) = F^{-1}(R4_i ⊕ F(R3_i⊕K3) ⊕ R0_i) ⊕ F^{-1}(R4_0 ⊕ F(R3_0⊕K3) ⊕ R0_0)

Wait let me redo:
K1 condition: h_i(K3) ⊕ r1_i(K0) = K1 for all i, where h_i = F^{-1}(R2_i ⊕ R0_i) and r1_i = PL_i ⊕ F(R0_i⊕K0).

Actually r2_i = R4_i ⊕ F(R3_i ⊕ K3), so h_i(K3) = F^{-1}(r2_i ⊕ R0_i) = F^{-1}(R4_i ⊕ F(R3_i⊕K3) ⊕ R0_i).

Condition: h_i ⊕ r1_i = K1 constant → h_i ⊕ r1_i = h_0 ⊕ r1_0.
→ h_i ⊕ h_0 = r1_i ⊕ r1_0.
→ r1_i ⊕ r1_0 = [PL_i ⊕ PL_0] ⊕ [F(R0_i⊕K0) ⊕ F(R0_0⊕K0)].
So the condition is:
F(R0_i⊕K0) ⊕ F(R0_0⊕K0) ⊕ PL_i ⊕ PL_0 = h_i(K3) ⊕ h_0(K3) ∀i.

Define for K0: A_i(K0) = F(R0_i⊕K0) ⊕ F(R0_0⊕K0) ⊕ PL_i ⊕ PL_0.
Define for K3: B_i(K3) = h_i(K3) ⊕ h_0(K3) = F^{-1}(R4_i ⊕ F(R3_i⊕K3) ⊕ R0_i) ⊕ F^{-1}(R4_0 ⊕ F(R3_0⊕K3) ⊕ R0_0).

Then K1-consistency ⇔ A_i(K0) = B_i(K3) for all i (or a chosen subset of pairs).

Similarly, K2-consistency gives another coupling:
K2 condition: r2_i ⊕ F^{-1}(r1_i ⊕ R3_i) = K2 constant.
Let p_i(K0) = F^{-1}(r1_i ⊕ R3_i) where r1_i = PL_i⊕F(R0_i⊕K0). Condition: r2_i(K3) ⊕ p_i(K0) = const → r2_i ⊕ p_i = r2_0 ⊕ p_0 → p_i ⊕ p_0 = r2_i ⊕ r2_0.
r2_i(K3) = R4_i ⊕ F(R3_i ⊕ K3). So r2_i ⊕ r2_0 = [R4_i ⊕ R4_0] ⊕ [F(R3_i⊕K3) ⊕ F(R3_0⊕K3)].
Condition: p_i(K0) ⊕ p_0(K0) ⊕ R4_i ⊕ R4_0 = F(R3_i⊕K3) ⊕ F(R3_0⊕K3) ∀i.

Define A'_i(K0) = p_i(K0) ⊕ p_0(K0) ⊕ R4_i ⊕ R4_0 and B'_i(K3) = F(R3_i⊕K3) ⊕ F(R3_0⊕K3). Then K2-consistency ⇔ A'_i(K0) = B'_i(K3) ∀i.

These are two separate couplings — both must hold for the true key. 

Now the MITM idea: Build a hash table for K3 side indexes B_i. For each K3, compute the 31-value vector B_i(K3) (i=1..31, relative to pair 0). Store in a hash map: vector_key → list of K3. Then for each K0, compute A_i vector and look it up to find K3 candidates satisfying K1-check. This finds (K0,K3) pairs satisfying K1 consistency in O(2^20) total (amortized). But then we need K2 consistency too.

But if K1 is consistent, we still need K2 consistency. We can filter in second pass: for surviving (K0,K3) candidates (which should be very few if we use enough pairs), check K2 consistency explicitly.

BUT there's a subtlety: single-pair matching A_i = B_i for all 31 pairs. The false-accept rate: for random K0, K3, A_i=B_i is prob 2^-32 per i; over all 31, essentially the candidate is unique. So we'll find very few (probably just the true one, or a couple). 

This is a O(2^20 + 2^20) algorithm! That's excellent. Let me flesh this out.

Details:
- pairs: for i = 0..31, we have P_i, C_i. From them: PL_i, PR_i, R0_i = PL_i ⊕ PR_i, R3_i = CL_i ⊕ CR_i, R4_i = CL_i.

- K3 enumeration: for each k3 in 0..2^20-1, K3 = expand(k3). Compute h_i = F^{-1}(R4_i ⊕ F(R3_i ⊕ K3) ⊕ R0_i) for all i (need F and F^-1). Compute B_i = h_i ⊕ h_0 for i=1..31. Hash B-vector → k3.

Actually for the hash key, we can use a subset of pairs to reduce memory: use i = 1, 2, 3 (three pairs) for the hash key: 96 bits. Memory: 2^20 entries × (96 bits key + 32-bit index) ≈ ok. But collisions: with 2^20 entries and 96-bit key, collisions rare. But we must handle them.

- K0 enumeration: for each k0, K0 = expand(k0). Compute r1_i = PL_i ⊕ F(R0_i ⊕ K0) for all i. Compute A_i = [F(R0_i⊕K0) ⊕ F(R0_0⊕K0)] ⊕ [PL_i ⊕ PL_0] = r1_i ⊕ r1_0. For each K0, compute A-vector using same subset of pairs (say i=1,2,3), look up in hash table to get candidate K3 values.

Wait, A_i = r1_i ⊕ r1_0 (since F(R0_i⊕K0) ⊕ F(R0_0⊕K0) ⊕ PL_i ⊕ PL_0 = (r1_i ⊕ PL_i... let me recheck: r1_i = PL_i ⊕ F(R0_i⊕K0). So r1_i ⊕ r1_0 = PL_i ⊕ F(R0_i⊕K0) ⊕ PL_0 ⊕ F(R0_0⊕K0) = [PL_i ⊕ PL_0] ⊕ [F(R0_i⊕K0) ⊕ F(R0_0⊕K0)]. Yes, A_i = r1_i ⊕ r1_0. 

And h_i = F^{-1}(r2_i ⊕ R0_i) with r2_i = R4_i ⊕ F(R3_i⊕K3).

So:
1. Enumerate K3 (2^20): compute r2_i for all i, then h_i = F^{-1}(r2_i ⊕ R0_i), then B_i = h_i ⊕ h_0 for i in pairs_subset. Hash B → k3.
2. Enumerate K0 (2^20): compute r1_i = PL_i ⊕ F(R0_i⊕K0), then A_i = r1_i ⊕ r1_0 for pairs_subset. Look up hash to find k3 candidates. For each candidate (K0,K3): verify K1-consistency across ALL pairs: h_i ⊕ r1_i = const. Then verify K2-consistency: p_i ⊕ r2_i = const where p_i = F^{-1}(r1_i ⊕ R3_i), r2_i from K3.

Then for the surviving (K0,K3), compute K1 and K2:
K1 = r1_0 ⊕ h_0
K2 = r2_0 ⊕ p_0.

Then we have all four keys. Verify by re-encrypting pairs.

This is a clean O(2^20) MITM attack! The key insight is splitting the search into K3 (from ciphertext end) and K0 (from plaintext end).

But wait — I should double check the correctness of the K1-consistency criterion derivation.

Given K0: r1_i = PL_i ⊕ F(R0_i ⊕ K0). Round 0 eq: R1_i = r1_i. ✓
Given K3: r2_i = R4_i ⊕ F(R3_i ⊕ K3). Round 3 eq: R2_i = R4_i ⊕ F(R3_i ⊕ K3). ✓ (from R4 = R2 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3) → R2 = R4 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3))

Round 1 eq: R2 = R0 ⊕ F(R1 ⊕ K1) → F(R1_i ⊕ K1) = r2_i ⊕ R0_i → R1_i ⊕ K1 = F^{-1}(r2_i ⊕ R0_i) = h_i → K1 = r1_i ⊕ h_i. For consistency K1 must be same for all pairs i. So K1-consistency: r1_i ⊕ h_i constant.

Round 2 eq: R3 = R1 ⊕ F(R2 ⊕ K2) → F(R2_i ⊕ K2) = R3_i ⊕ R1_i → R2_i ⊕ K2 = F^{-1}(r1_i ⊕ R3_i) = p_i → K2 = r2_i ⊕ p_i. Consistency: r2_i ⊕ p_i constant.

Yes! The derivations are right.

Now the MITM:
- K1-consistency: r1_i ⊕ h_i = const across i ⇔ (r1_i ⊕ r1_0) = (h_i ⊕ h_0) for all i. ✓ since r1_i ⊕ h_i = r1_0 ⊕ h_0 ⇔ r1_i ⊕ r1_0 = h_i ⊕ h_0.

So the hash join matches A_i = r1_i ⊕ r1_0 with B_i = h_i ⊕ h_0. 

This is beautiful: For the true (K0, K3), A_i = B_i for every pair, and K1 = r1_0 ⊕ h_0 will be a consistent key. Similarly K2 will be consistent automatically (a second check we can apply to filter).

Note: even for the TRUE K0 and TRUE K3, the consistency of K1 holds by construction (that's how the cipher works). So the true pair will appear in the hash match. The question: how many false (K0,K3) also match on the subset of pairs (i=1..3)? The vector has 96 bits for 3 pairs, so expected false matches = (2^20 × 2^20) / 2^96 ≈ 2^-56 ≈ 0. So essentially only the true combination matches — but computationally we must iterate carefully.

Actually wait: for each K0 (2^20), A-vector (96-bit) computed, we look up matching K3 in table. The table has 2^20 entries. Since vectors essentially unique, each K0 lookup returns ~0 or 1 K3. So total work ~2^20 lookups + building 2^20 table. Great, O(2^20) with ~4MB-ish memory table.

But CAUTION: the hash table keyed by the 3-pair B-vector: with only 3 pairs (96 bits), 2^20 entries → very sparse, good.

Steps in C:
1. Read pairs, load P[32], C[32].
2. Compute for each i: PL, PR, R0 = PL^PR, R3 = CL^CR, R4 = CL.
3. Enumerate k3 in [0, 2^20):
   K3 = expand(k3)
   for i in 0..31:
     r2[i] = R4[i] ^ f(R3[i] ^ K3)
     h[i] = inv_f(r2[i] ^ R0[i])
   B = h[1]^h[0], h[2]^h[0], h[3]^h[0]  (subset for key)
   Insert into hash table keyed by (B1,B2,B3) → k3 (store list).
   Also we need h values later for candidate verification → store h? We can recompute when needed, or store all h for each k3? Storing h for all k3 (32 values × 2^20) = 128MB — maybe too much but possible. Better: recompute for surviving candidates (very few).
   Actually for the K1 verification, we need h_i for all pairs for candidate k3. We can recompute: h_i = inv_f(r2_i ^ R0_i) with r2_i recomputed from k3. That's a few F-ops per candidate. Since candidates are ~0-2, fine.

   Hash table: we need to store k3 values. Multiple k3 could map to same B (rare). Use chaining.

4. Enumerate k0 in [0, 2^20):
   K0 = expand(k0)
   for i: r1[i] = PL[i] ^ f(R0[i] ^ K0)
   A = (r1[1]^r1[0], r1[2]^r1[0], r1[3]^r1[0])
   look up in hash table → candidate k3 values
   for each candidate k3:
     recompute r2, h for all 32 pairs (or at least verify K1 and K2 consistency):
       K1 = r1[0] ^ h[0]
       check r1[i] ^ h[i] == K1 for all i
       K2 = r2[0] ^ p[0], p[i] = inv_f(r1[i] ^ R3[i])
       check r2[i] ^ p[i] == K2 for all i
     if both pass → we found keys (k0,k3,K1,K2). But we need the SEEDS for K1,K2 too! K1 and K2 derived here are EXPANDED 32-bit keys. We need to convert back to 20-bit seeds k1, k2. Since expand may be injective, find k1,k2 by checking expand(k1)==K1. Actually K1 and K2 derived should equal expand(seed1), expand(seed2) for the true seeds. We can find them by brute force (2^20 each) or maintain a reverse map of expand. Build reverse map expand⁻¹ once: for k in 0..2^20, rev[expand(k)] = k (if injective; handle collisions).
   
   Then verify full key by encrypting several pairs (or all).

Now I can proceed with the meet-in-the-middle attack on the real pairs. Let me write the C code.

Plan (C):
1. Read pairs.
2. Compute R0[i], R3[i], R4[i], PL[i] for each pair.
3. Build hash table over k3:
   - For each k3 in 0..0xFFFFF:
     - K3 = expand(k3)
     - For each pair i: r2[i] = R4[i] ^ f(R3[i] ^ K3); h[i] = f_inv(r2[i] ^ R0[i]).
     - Compute B vector relative to pair 0: for the chosen subset S (pairs 1,2,3): B_j = h[j] ^ h[0].
     - Insert (B-vector key, k3) into hash table.
4. Enumerate k0:
   - K0 = expand(k0)
   - For each pair i: r1[i] = PL[i] ^ f(R0[i] ^ K0).
   - A_j = r1[j] ^ r1[0] for j in S.
   - Look up (A-vector) in hash table → list of k3.
   - For each candidate k3:
     - recompute r2[i] and h[i] for all pairs (given k3).
     - K1 = r1[0] ^ h[0]; check all i: r1[i] ^ h[i] == K1.
     - p[i] = f_inv(r1[i] ^ R3[i]); K2 = r2[0] ^ p[0]; check all i: r2[i] ^ p[i] == K2.
     - If passes, found (k0, k3, K1, K2). Find seeds k1,k2 s.t. expand(k1)==K1, expand(k2)==K2.
5. Verify with full pairs encryption; write verdict.

Now I need to be careful with:
- The expand function: expand(k) = k ^ (k<<16) ^ (k*12345). In C, k is uint32, 32-bit. k*12345 could overflow? k up to 0xFFFFF (1,048,575) × 12345 = ~1.29e10 < 2^32 (4.29e9)? Let me check: 1,048,575 × 12,345 = 12,944,657,375 ≈ 1.29e10 > 4.29e9. So uint32 overflow does occur, but that's fine as it's supposed to be uint32 arithmetic per feal.c which uses uint32_t. Feal.c uses `key * 12345` on uint32_t key — matches. OK.

- Hash table: I'll implement open addressing with a power-of-two table. Key is 96 bits (three uint32). Table size: 2^20 entries is fine; to reduce collisions maybe 2^21. Each entry: uint64 hash key lower, plus k3. Actually simplest: use chaining with a hash computed from the 3 values.

Simplest robust approach: build a hash table: 
- Hash function: mix the three uint32s into a 64-bit value. Use a simple hash, e.g. h = (x*0x9E3779B97F4A17C5 ...). 
- Open addressing with linear probing: table slots store (key0, key1, key2, k3). Since expected occupancy 2^20 / 2^21 = 0.5, probes short.

Actually even simpler: since we need to look up each k0 in the table, and keys are 96-bit, I'll just use an open-address table with a good hash and store full key+value, with linear probing. Collision resolution by probe.

Implementation detail: for pair subset use pairs 1,2,3 (S={1,2,3}). 96 bits.

For finding k1,k2 seeds from expanded keys K1, K2: maintain a reverse lookup table rev[2^20] mapping expand(k) → k. Since expand might not be injective on 20-bit inputs, use a hash map or just brute force over 2^20 per found key (2^21 iterations total, negligible).

Alternatively simpler: after determining K1 and K2 (32-bit), brute force k1 over [0,2^20) checking expand(k1) == K1holiday — 2^20 fast. Do for both.

## Attack method (implemented in `/app/mitm_attack.c`)

A **meet-in-the-middle known-plaintext attack** that avoids the naive 2^40 search:

1. From each pair, the ciphertext fixes the round-4 state (`R4 = CL`, `R3 = CL⊕CR`) and the plaintext fixes the round-0 state (`L0 = PL`, `R0 = PL⊕PR`).
2. Using the fact that F is a known bijection:
   - Guessing **K3** (2^20) determines the internal `R2` for every pair via `R2 = R4 ⊕ F(R3⊕K3)`.
   - Guessing **K0** (2^20) determines the internal `R1` for every pair via `R1 = PL ⊕ F(R0⊕K0)`.
3. Round 1 and 2 then yield, per pair:
   - `K1 = R1 ⊕ F⁻¹(R2⊕R0)`
   - `K2 = R2 ⊕ F⁻¹(R1⊕R3)`
   
   These must be **constant across all 32 pairs** — two strong consistency checks.
4. **Meet-in-the-middle:** a hash table over all 2^20 K3 values stores the cross-pair difference vector `(h1⊕h0, h2⊕h0, h3⊕h0)` of the K1 derivation. Enumerating the 2^20 K0 values and looking up the matching vector finds the unique consistent `(K0, K3)` pair; K1/K2 are then derived directlyholiday and their seeds recovered by inverting `expand`.

Runtime: ~1 second, only ~2^20 F-evaluations — no 2^40 brute force required.

- The recovered key was verified by re-encrypting **all 32 known plaintext-ciphertext pairs** — all match.
- `decrypt` with these seeds reproduces the 32 known plaintexts exactly.
- The 100 ciphertexts in `/app/ciphertexts.txt` were decrypted to `/app/plaintexts.txt` (100 lines), and every decrypted plaintext re-encrypts to its original ciphertext (0 mismatches), confirming correctness.
